\mathcal{E}_{k}:=\{H:\sigma_{2d-k}(H)\geq c_{0}\sqrt{n}/16\text{ and }\sigma_{2d-k+1}(H)\leq c_{0}\sqrt{n}/16\},

so that exactly one event from
\mathcal{E}_{k},k\in[0,2d]
holds for any given
H
.

For any
(N,N_{1},\kappa,\kappa^{\prime},D_{1},D_{2},D_{3})
box pair
(\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2})
, we specify a subset of atypical vectors
\operatorname{AT}(\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2})
as the following subset of
\mathcal{B}_{1}\times\mathcal{B}_{2}
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{AT}=

\displaystyle\operatorname{AT}(\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2})=\{(X,Y)\in\mathcal{B}_{1}\times\mathcal{B}_{2}:\operatorname{LCD}^{N^{-1},N_{1}^{-1}}_{\frac{8\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{\nu p}}+\frac{256\sqrt{2}B^{2}}{\sqrt{c_{0}}},c_{\rho,\delta}^{2}c_{0}^{4}2^{-16}}

\displaystyle(c_{0}2^{-5}n^{-1/2}X_{D},c_{0}2^{-5}n^{-1/2}Y_{D})\leq 2^{11}B^{2},|\sin(X_{D},Y_{D})|\geq c_{\rho,\delta}^{2}c_{0}^{2}/128\}.

Those
(X,Y)
not satisfying the lower bound on
|\sin(X_{D},Y_{D})|
are not placed in
\operatorname{AT}
.

For any given fixed
L>0
we take
\alpha=\alpha(L)
to be the constant satisfying

(3.9)

(10^{12}\alpha(L)\cdot(2+\frac{40}{c_{0}\kappa_{0}\kappa})^{2})^{c_{0}^{2}n/16}=(\frac{1}{2L^{4}})^{n},

so that

\alpha(L)=L^{-64/c_{0}^{2}}\cdot\text{(a function of $c_{0}$ and $B$)}.

As the exact dependence of this function on
c_{0}
and
B
is involved and not informative enough, we will not work out the explicit expression of this function.

By Corollary
3.6
(recall that
\nu=2^{-15}
and
p\geq 1/(2^{7}B^{4})
), there exists a function
V_{\ref{corollaryofthelcd}}(B,c_{0})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
depending only on
B
and
c_{0}
such that whenever

(3.10)

N\leq\exp(L^{-64/c_{0}^{2}}\cdot V_{\ref{corollaryofthelcd}}(B,c_{0})\cdot n),

we have

\mathbb{P}_{X,Y}((X,Y)\in\operatorname{AT})\leq(\frac{1}{2L^{4}})^{n}.

Throughout the following, we assume that
n
is large enough relative to the constants
L,c_{0}
so that
\alpha(L)\cdot d\geq\alpha(L)c_{0}^{2}n/4\geq 4
, justifying a condition made in Corollary
3.6
.

We complete the double counting procedure in the following lemma:

Lemma 3.12
.

There exists a function
V_{\ref{lemmafinaltwomoments}}(B,c_{0})>0
depending only on
B>1
and
c_{0}\in(0,1)
such that the following holds. Fix some
c\in[-2,2]
and two integers
N\geq N_{1}\geq 2
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying

N\leq\exp(L^{-64/c_{0}^{2}}V_{\ref{lemmafinaltwomoments}}(B,c_{0})n)
. Let
X,Y
be uniformly sampled from a
(N,N_{1},\kappa,\kappa^{\prime},D_{1},D_{2},D_{3})
box. Then whenever
n
is sufficiently large relative to
L,c_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{X,Y}

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{P}_{M_{n}}(\|(M_{n}X,M_{n}(cX+Y))\|_{2}\leq n)\geq(\frac{L^{2}}{NN_{1}})^{n},|\sin(X_{D},Y_{D})|\geq\frac{c_{\rho,\delta}^{2}c_{0}^{2}}{128}\right)

\displaystyle\leq(\frac{R_{2}}{L^{2}})^{2n}

where
R_{2}=C_{B}c_{0}^{-16}
and
C_{B}>0
is a constant depending only on
B
.

Proof.

Let the events
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathcal{E}_{k}
be defined as above.
We denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}:=

\displaystyle\{(X,Y)\in\mathcal{B}_{1}\times\mathcal{B}_{2}:\quad|\sin(X_{D},Y_{D})|\geq c_{\rho,\delta}^{2}c_{0}^{2}/128,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{M_{n}}(\|(M_{n}X,M_{n}(cX+Y))\|_{2}\leq n)\geq(L^{2}/NN_{1})^{n}\}.

Then we can write

\mathbb{P}_{X,Y}(\mathcal{E})\leq\mathbb{P}_{X,Y}((X,Y)\in\operatorname{AT})+\mathbb{P}_{X,Y}(\mathcal{E}\cap\{(X,Y)\notin\operatorname{AT}\}).

We also define the set of typical directions for
(X,Y)\in\mathcal{B}_{1}\times\mathcal{B}_{2}
:

T=\{(X,Y):\operatorname{LCD}^{N^{-1},N_{1}^{-1}}_{\frac{8\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{\nu p}}+\frac{256\sqrt{2}B^{2}}{\sqrt{c_{0}}},c_{\rho,\delta}^{2}c_{0}^{4}2^{-16}}(c_{0}2^{-5}n^{-1/2}X_{D},c_{0}2^{-5}n^{-1/2}Y_{D})\geq 2^{11}B^{2}\},

and define a two variable function

\displaystyle f(X,Y)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{P}_{M}(\|(M_{n}X,M_{n}(cX+Y))\|_{2}\leq n)\cdot\mathbf{1}((X,Y)\in T).

Then by the above reasoning and Markov’s inequality we have

(3.11)

\mathbb{P}_{X,Y}(\mathcal{E})\leq\mathbb{P}_{X,Y}(f(X,Y)\geq(\frac{L^{2}}{NN_{1}})^{n})+(\frac{1}{2L^{4}})^{n}\leq(\frac{N^{2}N_{1}^{2}}{L^{4}})^{n}\mathbb{E}_{X,Y}f(X,Y)^{2}+(\frac{1}{2L^{4}})^{n}.

By Fact
3.11
and a further partitioning, we have the decomposition

(3.12)

\mathbb{P}_{M_{n}}(\|(M_{n}X,M_{n}(cX+Y)\|_{2}\leq n)^{2}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{2d}\mathbb{P}_{H}(\mathcal{A}_{2}\mid\mathcal{A}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{k})\mathbb{P}_{H}(\mathcal{A}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{k}),

so that

(3.13)

f(X,Y)^{2}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{2d}\mathbb{P}_{H}(\mathcal{A}_{2}\mid\mathcal{A}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{k})\mathbb{P}_{H}(\mathcal{A}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{k})\mathbf{1}((X,Y)\in T).

We first take
k\leq 2^{-21}B^{-4}d
. Then by Theorem
3.7
, we get that whenever
N^{2}\leq R^{2}\exp(-2^{-23}B^{-4}d)
where the constant
R
is specified in Theorem
3.7
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{k})\leq\exp(-c_{0}nk/12)(\frac{R_{0}^{2}}{NN_{1}})^{2n-2d},

where
R_{0}=2^{70}B^{4}c_{0}^{-8}c_{\rho,\delta}^{-2}
(we used
p\geq 1/(2^{7}B^{4})
and
\nu=2^{-15}
). (More precisely,
\mathcal{A}_{1}
is the event where
\|H_{1}X_{D}/3\|_{2},\|H_{1}Y_{D}/3\|_{2},\|H_{2}X_{D}/3\|_{2},\|H_{2}Y_{D}/3\|_{2}\leq n
, and by assumption the essential LCD (for abbreviation, let
L_{*},\alpha_{*}
denote the parameters we have used for the LCD of
X_{D},Y_{D}
) satisfies
\operatorname{LCD}_{L_{*},\alpha_{*}}^{N^{-1},N_{1}^{-1}}(X_{D},Y_{D})\geq 2^{11}B^{2}
, so that
\operatorname{LCD}_{L_{*},\alpha_{*}}^{3/N,3/N_{1}}(X_{D}/3,Y_{D}/3)\geq 512B^{2}
, and we can apply Theorem
3.7
with the new parameter
\mathbf{t}=(3/N,3/N_{1})
).

Now we consider
\mathbb{P}_{(X,Y),H}(\mathcal{A}_{2}\mid\mathcal{A}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{k})
. Define
g_{k}(X,Y):=\mathbb{P}_{H}(\mathcal{A}_{2}\mid\mathcal{A}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{k})
. Then we have

\mathbb{E}_{X,Y}[g_{k}(X,Y)]=\mathbb{E}_{X_{[d]},Y_{[d]}}\mathbb{E}_{H}[\mathbb{E}_{X_{[d+1,n]},Y_{[d+1,n]}}\mathbf{1}[\mathcal{A}_{2}]]\mid\mathcal{A}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{k}].

For any given
H\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{k}
where
k\leq 2^{-21}B^{-4}d
, we have
\sigma_{2d-k}(H)\geq c_{0}\sqrt{n}/16
, so that by Lemma
3.10
and by the independence of
X
and
Y
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X_{[d+1,n]},Y_{[d+1,n]}}\mathbf{1}(\mathcal{A}_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{X_{[d+1,n]}}(\|H^{T}X_{[d+1,n]}\|_{2}\leq 6n)\mathbb{P}_{Y_{[d+1,n]}}(\|H^{T}Y_{[d+1,n]}\|_{2}\leq 6n)

\displaystyle\leq(\frac{C^{\prime}n}{c_{0}dN})^{2d-k}(\frac{C^{\prime}n}{c_{0}dN_{1}})^{2d-k}\leq(\frac{16C^{\prime 2}}{c_{0}^{6}NN_{1}})^{2d-k},

where
C^{\prime}>0
is an absolute constant. We now take
R_{1}=\max(16C^{\prime 2}c_{0}^{-6},R_{0}^{2})
.

Finally, we consider
k\geq 2^{-21}B^{-4}d:=\alpha^{\prime}d
. In this case, we have (where
\alpha^{\prime}=2^{-21}B^{-4})
:

\sum_{k=2^{-21}B^{-4}d}^{2d}\mathbb{P}_{H}(\mathcal{A}_{2}\mid\mathcal{A}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{k})\mathbb{P}_{H}(\mathcal{A}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{k})\leq\mathbb{P}(\sigma_{2d-\alpha^{\prime}d}(H)\leq c_{0}\sqrt{n}/16)\leq\exp(-c_{0}\alpha^{\prime}dn/12).

Combining all the above cases, we conclude that

\mathbb{E}_{X,Y}f(X,Y)^{2}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha^{\prime}d}\exp(-c_{0}kn/12)(\frac{R_{1}}{NN_{1}})^{2n-k}+\exp(-c_{0}\alpha^{\prime}dn/12).

When
N
is not too large in the sense that
(R_{1}/N)^{2}\geq\max(\exp(-c_{0}n/12),\exp(-c_{0}\alpha^{\prime}d/24))
, then we have that

\mathbb{E}_{X,Y}f(X,Y)^{2}\leq(\frac{2R_{1}}{NN_{1}})^{2n}.

Taking this estimate into (
3.11
) completes the proof.

Throughout the proof there are three places where we placed an upper bound on the size of
N
. The first is in (
3.10
), the second is when we take
N^{2}\leq R^{2}\exp(-2^{-23}B^{-4}d)
and the third is in the last paragraph. We take the intersection of all these constraints into the following condition: for any
L\geq 1
we require that
N\leq\exp(L^{-64/c_{0}^{2}}V_{\ref{lemmafinaltwomoments}}(B,c_{0})n)
where
V_{\ref{lemmafinaltwomoments}}(B,c_{0})>0
is a function that depends only on
B
and
c_{0}
.

∎

Now we are ready to complete the proof of Theorem
3.8
.

Proof of Theorem
3.8
.

We apply Lemma
3.3
with
N=\kappa_{0}/4\epsilon,N_{1}=\kappa_{0}\epsilon_{1}/4\epsilon
and write

\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)\subseteq\cup_{(\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2})\in\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)}((4\epsilon n^{-1/2})\cdot(\mathcal{B}_{1},c\mathcal{B}_{1}+\mathcal{B}_{2}))\cap\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)

where
({B}_{1},c\mathcal{B}_{1}+\mathcal{B}_{2})
denotes
\{(X,cX+Y):X\in\mathcal{B}_{1},Y\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
}. Thus

|\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)|\leq|\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)|\max_{(\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2})\in\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)}|(4\epsilon n^{-1/2}\cdot(\mathcal{B}_{1},c\mathcal{B}_{1}+\mathcal{B}_{2}))\cap\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)|.

Multiplying by
\sqrt{n}/4\epsilon
, we get from Lemma
3.12
that

\displaystyle|(4\epsilon n^{-1/2}\cdot(\mathcal{B}_{1},c\mathcal{B}_{1}+\mathcal{B}_{2}))\cap\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)|

\displaystyle\leq\{(X,Y)\in\mathcal{B}_{1}\times\mathcal{B}_{2}:\mathbb{P}_{M_{n}}(\|(M_{n}X,M_{n}(cX+Y))\|_{2}\leq n\}\geq(L^{2}\epsilon^{2}/\epsilon_{1})^{n}\}|\leq(\frac{R_{2}^{\prime}}{L^{2}})^{2n}|\mathcal{B}|,

where
R_{2}^{\prime}
is
R_{2}
multiplied by a constant depending on
\kappa_{0}
and our upper bound on
\log\epsilon^{-1}
(
\log\epsilon^{-1}\leq V_{\ref{mainresultchapter3}}(B,c_{0})L^{-64/c_{0}^{2}}n
for a suitable given function
V_{\ref{mainresultchapter3}}(B,c_{0})>0
) yields the necessary upper bound on
\log N
required in the application of Lemma
3.12
.

By Lemma
3.3
we have
|\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)|\leq\kappa^{n}
, and that
|\mathcal{B}_{1}|\leq(\kappa N)^{n},|\mathcal{B}_{2}|\leq(\kappa N_{1})^{n}
. Thus

|\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)|\leq\kappa^{n}(\frac{R_{2}^{\prime}}{L^{2}})^{2n}\kappa^{2n}N^{n}N_{1}^{n}\leq(\frac{C_{B}}{c_{0}^{16}L^{4}\epsilon^{2}/\epsilon_{1}})^{n}

for a constant
C_{B}>0
depending only on
B
. This completes the proof.
∎

We also sketch the proof for Proposition
3.9
for completeness:

Proof of Proposition
3.9
(sketch).

By definition of
\Lambda_{\epsilon}^{0}
, the two vectors in
\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,0}
are the same. It suffices to prove the claimed upper bound holds for the cardinality of the following set

(3.14)

\{v\in\Lambda_{\epsilon}^{0}:\mathbb{P}(\|Mv\|_{2}\leq 4\epsilon\sqrt{n})\geq(L_{0}^{2}\epsilon)^{n}\}.

This is proven in
[
5
]
, Theorem III.2 (more precisely, although the definition of
\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon}
involves one extra condition, the proof for the upper bound for
|\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon}|
only uses the condition in (
3.14
)), with the constant
L
there in place of
L_{0}^{2}
here. It suffices to plug in the expression of
L_{0}
.

∎

3.4.
Verifying properties of a net

We need a simple Fourier replacement lemma that compares the anticoncentration with respect to
M_{n}
to the anticoncentration with respect to
A_{n}
. The following lemma is very similar to
[
6
]
, Lemma 8.1 and
[
18
]
, Lemma 3.24 and thus we omit the straightforward proof.

Lemma 3.13
.

For any
L>0
,
\epsilon_{1}>0
,
t_{0}>0
and any
(v,w)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
satisfying that

\mathbb{P}(\|(M_{n}v,M_{n}w)\|_{2}\leq t\sqrt{n})\leq(4L^{2}t^{2}/\epsilon_{1})^{n}\quad\forall t\geq t_{0},\quad\text{(resp. $(4L^{2}t)^{n}\quad\forall t\geq t_{0}$) },

we must have,

\mathcal{L}\left((A_{n}v,A_{n}w),t_{0}\sqrt{n}\right)\leq(50L^{2}t_{0}^{2}/\epsilon_{1})^{n},\quad\text{(resp. $(50L^{2}t_{0})^{n}$)},

where
(A_{n}v,A_{n}w)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
has its first (resp. last) n coordinates
A_{n}v
(resp.,
A_{n}w
).

The following proposition shows that
\Sigma_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)
serves as a net:

Proposition 3.14
.

Assume that
2^{16}B^{4}d\leq n
.
Let
\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}>0
satisfy that

(3.15)

\epsilon,\frac{\epsilon}{\epsilon_{1}}\in(0,\min(\kappa_{0}/8,c_{\rho,\delta}^{4}c_{0}^{4}2^{-22})).

Then for any
(v,w)\in\Sigma_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)
with
w=cv+\epsilon_{1}r
there exists
(v^{\prime},w^{\prime})=(v^{\prime},cv^{\prime}+\epsilon_{1}r^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)
with
\|v-v^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq 4\epsilon n^{-1/2}
,
\|r-r^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq 4\epsilon\epsilon_{1}^{-1}n^{-1/2}
and
\|w-w^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq 12\epsilon n^{-1/2}
.

Proof.

First, it is not hard to check that a necessary condition for the set
\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{1}}(c)
and
\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon,0}(c)
in Definition
3.1
to be nonempty is that
c\in[-\sqrt{2},\sqrt{2}]
.

For a given
(v,w)=(v,cv+\epsilon_{1}r)\in\Sigma_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)
we define two random vectors
t^{1}=(t_{1}^{1},\cdots,t_{n}^{1})
and
t^{2}=(t_{1}^{2},\cdots,t_{n}^{2})
with mutually independent coordinates, where
\mathbb{E}t_{i}^{1}=\mathbb{E}t_{i}^{2}=0,i\in[n]
,
|t_{i}^{1}|\leq 4\epsilon n^{-1/2}
,
|t_{i}^{2}|\leq 4\epsilon/\epsilon_{1}\cdot n^{-1/2}
and
v-t^{1}\in 4\epsilon n^{-1/2}\cdot\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
r-t^{2}\in 4\epsilon/\epsilon_{1}n^{-1/2}\cdot\mathbb{Z}^{n}
almost surely. We define
v^{\prime}=v-t^{1}
and
r^{\prime}=r-t^{2}
, and write
w^{\prime}=cv^{\prime}+\epsilon_{1}r^{\prime}
. Then by definition,
\|v-v^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq 4\epsilon n^{-1/2}
and
\|w-w^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq 4\epsilon\epsilon_{1}^{-1}n^{-1/2}
, and we have
\|w-w^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq(1+|c|)4\epsilon n^{-1/2}\leq 12\epsilon n^{-1/2}
. Also, from the fact that
(v,r)\in\mathcal{I}(D)
, our assumption on
\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}
and Fact
2.13
, we can see that
(v^{\prime},r^{\prime})\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}(D)
almost surely.

Then we only need to check the conditions imposed on the threshold function. That is, we show there exists a
(v^{\prime},w^{\prime})
such that

(3.16)

\mathbb{P}(\|(M_{n}v^{\prime},M_{n}w^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq 4\epsilon\sqrt{n})\geq(L^{2}\epsilon^{2}/\epsilon_{1})^{n},\quad\mathcal{L}_{A,op}(v^{\prime},w^{\prime},\epsilon\sqrt{n})\leq(2^{18}L^{2}\epsilon^{2}/\epsilon_{1})^{n}.

We first check that the second condition is satisfied by all pairs
(v^{\prime},w^{\prime})
in this construction. Write
\mathcal{K}:=\{\|A_{n}\|_{op}\leq 4\sqrt{n}\}
and let
\tau(v^{\prime}),\tau(w^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be such that

(3.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{A,op}(v^{\prime},w^{\prime},\epsilon\sqrt{n}):

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\left\|\begin{bmatrix}A_{n}v-A_{n}t^{1}-\tau(v^{\prime})\\
A_{n}w-A_{n}(ct^{1}+\epsilon t^{2})-\tau(w^{\prime})\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{2}\leq\epsilon\sqrt{n}\text{ and }\mathcal{K})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\left\|\begin{bmatrix}A_{n}v-\tau(v^{\prime})\\
A_{n}w-\tau(w^{\prime})\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{2}\leq 18\epsilon\sqrt{n})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{L}_{A}(v,w,18\epsilon\sqrt{n})\leq(2^{18}L^{2}\epsilon^{2}/\epsilon_{1})^{n}

where we use the assumption
(v,w)\in\Sigma_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)
combined with Lemma
3.13
.

Then we check the first condition of (
3.16
) holds for some
(v^{\prime},w^{\prime})
. Define the event
\mathcal{E}:=\{M_{n}:\|(M_{n}v,M_{n}w)\|_{2}\leq 2\epsilon\sqrt{n}\}
. Then for all
(v^{\prime},w^{\prime})
we have

\mathbb{P}_{M_{n}}(\|(M_{n}v^{\prime},M_{n}w^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq 4\epsilon\sqrt{n})\geq\mathbb{P}_{M_{n}}(\|(M_{n}t^{1},cM_{n}t^{1}+\epsilon_{1}M_{n}t^{2})\|_{2}\leq 2\epsilon\sqrt{n}\text{ and }\mathcal{E}).

Taking expectations with respect to
t=(t^{1},t^{2})
, we get

(3.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{t}\mathbb{P}_{M_{n}}(\|(M_{n}v^{\prime},M_{n}w^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq 4\epsilon\sqrt{n})

\displaystyle\geq(1-\mathbb{E}_{t}\mathbb{P}_{M_{n}}(\|(M_{n}t^{1},cM_{n}t^{1}+\epsilon_{1}M_{n}t^{2})\|_{2}\geq 2\epsilon\sqrt{n}\mid\mathcal{E}))\mathbb{P}_{M_{n}}(\|(M_{n}v,M_{n}w)\|_{2}\leq 2\epsilon\sqrt{n}).

Then we will prove, after exchanging the order of the expectation, that

(3.19)

\mathbb{E}_{M_{n}}[\mathbb{P}_{t}(\|(M_{n}t^{1},cM_{n}t^{1}+\epsilon_{1}M_{n}t^{2})\|_{2}\geq 2\epsilon\sqrt{n})\mid\mathcal{E}]\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Now use the fact that
t_{i}
has zero mean and are independent, we use triangle inequality to expand the following sum as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{t}\|M_{n}t^{1}\|_{2}^{2}+\mathbb{E}_{t}\|cM_{n}t^{1}+\epsilon_{1}t^{2}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 3\mathbb{E}_{t}\|M_{n}t^{1}\|_{2}^{2}+2\epsilon_{1}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{t}\|M_{n}t^{2}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i}\mathbb{E}_{t}[3(t_{i}^{1})^{2}+2\epsilon_{1}^{2}(t_{i}^{2})^{2}]\sum_{j}M_{ij}^{2}\leq 100\epsilon^{2}n^{-1}\|M_{n}\|_{HS}^{2}\leq\epsilon^{2}n

where the last inequality follows from our assumption on
d
:
\|M_{n}\|_{HS}^{2}=\sum_{i,j}M_{ij}^{2}\leq 256B^{4}d(n-d)\leq n^{2}/100
.
This verifies (
3.19
) via a second moment computation.

Since
(v,w)\in\Sigma_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)
we have by definition of the threshold function
\tau_{L,\epsilon_{1}}(v,w)
that
\mathbb{P}_{M_{n}}(\|(M_{n}v,M_{n}w)\|_{2}\leq 2\epsilon\sqrt{n})\geq(2\epsilon^{2}L^{2}/\epsilon_{1})^{n}
.

Combining these estimates into (
3.18
), we have thus verified that

\mathbb{E}_{t}\mathbb{P}_{M_{n}}(\|(M_{n}v^{\prime},M_{n}w^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq 4\epsilon\sqrt{n})\geq(1/2)((2\epsilon^{2}L^{2}/\epsilon_{1})^{n}\geq(L^{2}\epsilon^{2}/\epsilon_{1})^{n},

so that the first estimate in (
3.16
) is satisfied by some
(v^{\prime},w^{\prime})
.

∎

Proposition
3.14
does not cover the range when
\epsilon_{1}/\epsilon
is not large enough. For this range, and for those
(v,w)\in\Sigma_{\epsilon,0}(c)
, we use a different argument to verify the net property:

Proposition 3.15
.

Assume that
2^{16}B^{4}d\leq n
.
Let
0<\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{1}
satisfy that
\epsilon\leq\kappa_{0}/8
and

\frac{\epsilon}{\epsilon_{1}}\in(\min(\kappa_{0}/8,c_{\rho,\delta}^{4}c_{0}^{4}2^{-22}),1).
Then for any
(v,w)\in\Sigma_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)
there exists
(v^{\prime},w^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon_{1},0}(c)
with
\|v-v^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq 4\epsilon_{1}n^{-1/2}
and
\|w-w^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq 12\epsilon_{1}n^{-1/2}
.

Similarly, for any
(v,w)\in\Sigma_{\epsilon,0}(c)
we can find
(v^{\prime},w^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,0}
with
\|v-v^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq 4\epsilon n^{-1/2}
,
\|w-w^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq 12\epsilon n^{-1/2}
.

Proof.

We only prove the first case, as the second case
(v,w)\in\Sigma_{\epsilon,0}(c)
can be obtained from a direct modification from
[
3
]
, Lemma 8.2 or from modifying the proof of Proposition
3.14
.

In the first case, we follow similar steps as in the proof of Proposition
3.14

but now define
t^{1},t^{2}
satisfying
\mathbb{E}t_{i}^{1}=\mathbb{E}t_{i}^{2}=0\forall i\in[n]
,
|t_{i}^{1}|\leq 4\epsilon_{1}n^{-1/2},|t_{i}^{2}|\leq 4n^{-1/2}
and that
v-t^{1}\in 4\epsilon_{1}n^{-1/2}\cdot\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
r-t^{2}\in 4n^{-1/2}\cdot\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Set
v^{\prime}=w^{\prime}=v-t^{1}
, then
\|v-v^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq 4\epsilon_{1}n^{-1/2}
and
\|w-w^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq 12\epsilon_{1}n^{-1/2}
. We have by construction that
(v^{\prime},w^{\prime})\in\Sigma_{\epsilon}^{0}
. Then we can prove that there exists a realization
(v^{\prime},w^{\prime})
satisfying that

(3.20)

\mathbb{P}(\|(M_{n}v^{\prime},M_{n}w^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq 4\epsilon_{1}\sqrt{n})\geq(L_{0}^{2}\epsilon_{1})^{n},\quad\mathcal{L}_{A,op}(v^{\prime},w^{\prime},\epsilon_{1}\sqrt{n})\leq(2^{18}L^{2}\epsilon_{1})^{n},

where to justify the second inequality we use the fact that
\tau_{L,\epsilon_{1}}(v,w)\leq\epsilon_{1}
and we use Lemma
3.13
to transfer this bound to an estimate on
\mathcal{L}_{A,op}
, using similar steps as in (
3.17
).

To justify the first inequality in (
3.20
), we can first prove that

(3.21)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{t}\mathbb{P}_{M_{n}}(\|(M_{n}v^{\prime},M_{n}w^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq 4\epsilon_{1}\sqrt{n})

\displaystyle\geq(1-\mathbb{E}_{t}\mathbb{P}_{M_{n}}(\|(M_{n}t^{1},cM_{n}t^{1}+\epsilon_{1}M_{n}t^{2})\|_{2}\geq 2\epsilon_{1}\sqrt{n}\mid\mathcal{E}))\mathbb{P}_{M_{n}}(\|(M_{n}v,M_{n}w)\|_{2}\leq 2\epsilon\sqrt{n}),

where
\mathcal{E}:=\{M_{n}:\|(M_{n}v,M_{n}w)\|_{2}\leq 2\epsilon\sqrt{n}\}
. Then via a similar second moment estimate and using the assumption
(v,w)\in\Sigma_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)
, we get that this is lower bounded by

(L^{2}\epsilon^{2}/\epsilon_{1})^{n}\geq(L_{0}^{2}\epsilon_{1})^{n}

using our definition of
L_{0}
. This verifies that
(v^{\prime},w^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,0}
and completes the proof.

We also note the following simple fact for future use:

Fact 3.16
.

Let
r\geq t\geq 0
and consider
X
a random variable in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then

\mathcal{L}(X,t)\leq\mathcal{L}(X,r)\leq(1+2r/t)^{n}\mathcal{L}(X,t).

This is because a ball of radius
r
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
can be covered by
(1+2r/t)^{n}
balls of radius
t
.

∎

4.
Verifying quasi-randomness conditions II

In this section we complete the proof of Theorem
2.6
, our main quasi-randomness theorem. The main input in its proof is Theorem
3.8
and Proposition
3.9
.

Recall that we need to show the subvector LCD
\hat{D}_{\alpha,\gamma,\mu}(v_{1},v_{2})
is large for some sufficiently small
\mu
. As this definition involves the vectors
v_{1},v_{2}
on only
(1-2\mu)n
coordinates, we first need to use the incompressibility condition to show that these restricted vectors are linearly independent when restricted to any interval
I\subset[n]
,
|I|=(1-2\mu)n
.

Fact 4.1
.

To prove Theorem
2.6
, we only need to prove the theorem for vector pairs
(v_{1},v_{2})
satisfying the conditions stated in (1)-(3) of Corollary
2.12
, and moreover satisfies the following condition:
v_{2}=cv_{1}+\epsilon_{1}v_{3}
for some
v_{3}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
orthogonal to
v_{1}
, and that for any
I\subset[n]
with
|I|\geq(1-2\mu)n
, we have the following estimates for all
\mu\in(0,\kappa_{3})
:

\|(v_{1})_{I}\|_{2},\|(v_{3})_{I}\|_{2}\geq\kappa_{4},\quad\text{ more generally }\forall\theta\in\mathbb{R},\|(\cos\theta v_{1}+\sin\theta v_{3})_{I}\|_{2}\geq\kappa_{4},

where
\kappa_{3},\kappa_{4}\in(0,1)
are two fixed constants depending only on
B
.

Proof.

We only need to work on the event stated in Lemma
2.5
, that is, with probability
1-\exp(-\Omega(n))
, any linear combination
\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{3}v_{3}
satisfying that
\theta_{1},\theta_{3}=O(1)
and
\|\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{3}v_{3}\|_{2}=1
is
(\delta,\rho)
- incompressible.
Now take any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, by orthogonality we have
\|\cos\theta v_{1}+\sin\theta v_{3}\|_{2}=1
. By the incompressibility event we work on,
\cos\theta v_{1}+\sin\theta v_{3}
is again
(\delta,\rho)
-incompressible. Then by Fact
2.7
, we can find
\kappa_{3},\kappa_{4}\in(0,1)
such that the lower bound on
\|(cos\theta v_{1}+\sin\theta v_{3})_{I}\|_{2}
holds for any such interval
I\subset[n]
with
|I|\geq(1-2\mu)n
.
∎

By fact
4.1
, for any interval
I
with
|I|\geq(1-2\mu)n
, let
C_{I}^{1},C_{I}^{3}
be any two constants that satisfy
\|(C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3})_{I}\|_{2}=1
, then we must have

(4.1)

\frac{1}{2}\leq\max(C_{I}^{1},C_{I}^{3})\leq 1/\kappa_{4}.

Reduction 4.2
.

(A family of reductions) We first let
c_{*}>0
be the constant given in Lemma
2.5
and we abbreviate
\hat{D}_{\alpha,\gamma,\mu}(v_{1},v_{2})
for
\hat{D}_{\alpha,\gamma,\mu}^{c_{*}}(v_{1},v_{2})
. Take
c_{\Sigma}\in(0,\min(\frac{c_{*}}{4},2^{-5}))
.

(1)

By Corollary
2.12
, we only need to consider those
(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{1}}(c)
for some
c,\epsilon_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\epsilon_{1}^{2}+c^{2}=1
, and those
(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{1}}^{0}(c)
for some
|\epsilon_{1}|\leq 1
. However, by our requirement
\|\operatorname{Proj}_{v_{1}^{\perp}}(v_{2})\|_{2}\geq e^{-c_{*}n/2}
in Theorem
2.6
, we only need to consider those subsets where
\epsilon_{1}\geq e^{-c_{*}n/2}
.

(2)

We can take a
2^{-n}
-net for
c,\epsilon_{1}
in item (1). Take any
(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{1}}(c)
with
c^{2}+\epsilon_{1}^{2}=1
, and take
c^{\prime},\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}
from the net such that
|c-c^{\prime}|\leq 2^{-n},|\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}|\leq 2^{-n}
. Then from the definition
v_{2}=cv_{1}+\epsilon r
for some
r\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
orthogonal to
v_{1}
, we define
v_{2}^{\prime}=c^{\prime}v_{1}+\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}r
and
v_{1}^{\prime}=v_{1}
. Then
(v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}}(c^{\prime})
and
\|v_{2}-v_{2}^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq 2^{-n+2}
.

(3)

By the definition of the subvector LCD
\hat{D}_{\alpha,\gamma,\mu}(v_{1},v_{2})
, to prove Theorem
2.6
we only need to rule out vector pairs
(v_{1},v_{3})
such that for some
I\subset[n]
,
|I|\geq(1-2\mu)n
and for some
C_{I}^{1},C_{I}^{3}
satisfying (
4.1
), we have
D_{\alpha,\gamma}((C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3})_{I})\leq e^{c_{\Sigma}n}
. That is, we do not need
C_{I}^{1},C_{I}^{3}
to satisfy the strict relation
\|(C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3})_{I}\|_{2}=1
.

(4)

We can take a
2^{-n}
-net for
C_{I}^{1},C_{I}^{3}
with (
4.1
) because the definition of LCD is stable under a small perturbation: let
{C_{I}^{1^{\prime}}},{C_{I}^{3^{\prime}}}
satisfy
|C_{I}^{1^{\prime}}-C_{I}^{1}|\leq 2^{-n},|C_{I}^{3^{\prime}}-C_{I}^{3}|\leq 2^{-n}
, then
D_{\alpha,\gamma}((C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3})_{I})\leq e^{c_{\Sigma}n}
implies that
D_{2\alpha,2\gamma}(C_{I}^{1^{\prime}}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3^{\prime}}v_{3})\leq e^{c_{\Sigma}n}
.

(5)

For similar reasons, it is not hard to check that if
\hat{D}_{\alpha,\gamma,\mu}(v_{1},v_{2})\leq e^{c_{\Sigma}n}
and
(v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime})
approximates
(v_{1},v_{2})
in item (2), then
\hat{D}_{2\alpha,2\gamma,\mu}(v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime})\leq e^{c_{\Sigma}n}
.

(6)

We take a union bound over all disjoint
D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}\subset[n]
of cardinality
c_{0}^{2}n/12
each.

We can now reduce the proof of Theorem
2.6
to the following:

Corollary 4.3
.

To prove Theorem
2.6
, we only need the following: let
\mathcal{K}
denote the event
\mathcal{K}:=\{\|A_{n}\|_{op}\leq 4\sqrt{n}\},
then we can find
\alpha,\gamma,c_{\Sigma}\in(0,1)
such that for any
w_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\epsilon_{1},c,I,C_{I}^{1},C_{I}^{3},s_{1},t_{1},s_{2},t_{2}}\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{K}}(\exists(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{1}}(c)\cup\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{1}}^{0}(c)\text{ associated with }D=[c_{0}^{2}n/4]:

\displaystyle D_{\alpha,\gamma}((C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3})_{I})\leq e^{c_{\Sigma}n},\|(A_{n}-s_{i}I_{n})v_{i}-t_{i}w_{0}\|_{2}\leq 2^{-n/2}\forall i=1,2)\leq 2^{-500n},

with the supremum over
1/2\leq c^{2}+\epsilon_{1}^{2}\leq 3/2
,
\epsilon_{1}\geq e^{-c_{*}n/2}
, over
I\subset[n]
with
|I|\geq(1-2\mu)n
, over
C_{I}^{1},C_{I}^{3}
satisfying (
4.1
), and over
s_{i}\in[-4\sqrt{n},4\sqrt{n}]
and
t_{i}\in[-8\sqrt{n},8\sqrt{n}]
, i=1,2.

This corollary follows from using the
2^{-n}
-net for
\epsilon_{1},c,C_{I}^{1},C_{I}^{3}
; using the
2^{-n}
-net for
s_{1},t_{1},s_{2},t_{2}
in Reduction
4.2
; taking the union of
D
; and finally using
\|A_{n}\|_{op}\leq 4\sqrt{n}
.

In the following we always let
\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{1}}(c)
and
\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{1}}^{0}(c)
be associated with
D=[c_{0}^{2}n/4]
. Next we rescale the norm of
C_{I}^{1},C_{I}^{3}
to prepare for the application of Theorem
3.8
.

Corollary 4.4
.

We may decrease the value of
c_{*}
in Lemma
2.5
so that
c_{*}<10^{-5}
.
Then to prove Theorem
2.6
, we only need to prove the following: we can find
\alpha,\gamma,c_{\Sigma}\in(0,1)
sufficiently small such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\epsilon_{1},c,I,C_{I}^{1},C_{I}^{3},s_{1},s_{2}}\sup_{w_{0}^{1},w_{0}^{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{K}}(\exists(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{1}}(c)\cup\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{1}}^{0}(c):D_{\alpha,\gamma}((C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3})_{I})\leq e^{c_{\Sigma}n},

\displaystyle\|(A_{n}-s_{1}I_{n})v_{1}-w_{0}^{1}\|_{2}\leq 2^{-n/3},\quad

\displaystyle\|(A_{n}-s_{2}I_{n})(\underline{C}_{I}^{1}v_{1}+\underline{C}_{I}^{3}v_{3})+(s_{2}-s_{1})\underline{C}_{I}^{1}v_{1}-w_{0}^{2}\|_{2}\leq 2^{-n/3})\leq 2^{-600n},

where we define
\underline{C}_{I}^{1}=\frac{C_{I}^{1}}{\sqrt{(C_{I}^{1})^{2}+(C_{I}^{3})^{2}}}
and
\underline{C}_{I}^{3}=\frac{C_{I}^{3}}{\sqrt{(C_{I}^{1})^{2}+(C_{I}^{3})^{2}}}
.

Proof.

The reduction follows from taking a reformulation of the small ball probability in Corollary
4.3
, whee we use the assumption
\epsilon_{1}\geq e^{-10^{-5}n}
and we use the two-sided bound in (
4.1
) which gives
|C_{I}^{1}|,|C_{I}^{3}|\leq 1/\kappa_{4}
so we can for instance bound
\mathcal{L}(\epsilon_{1}v_{3},2^{-n/2})\leq\mathcal{L}({\underline{C}}_{I}^{3}v_{3},2^{-n/3})
. We also replace the supremum over
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R},w_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
by the supremum over any two vectors
w_{0}^{1},w_{0}^{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

∎

From now on, we fix a small enough value of
c_{0}\in(0,1)
so that Theorem
3.8
and Proposition
3.9
can be applied, and we fix
c_{*}
satisfying Lemma
2.5
such that
c_{*}\in(0,10^{-5})
. Let
\eta=\exp(-c_{\Sigma}n)
for some
c_{\Sigma}>0
small enough and take
j_{0}
be the largest integer such that
2^{j_{0}}\eta\leq\kappa_{0}/8
.

The probability in Corollary
4.4
will be evaluated in two steps. In the first step we consider vector pairs
(v_{1},v_{3})
where the threshold function
\tau_{L,\underline{C}_{I}^{3}}(v_{1},\underline{C}_{I}^{1}v_{1}+\underline{C}_{I}^{3}v_{3})\geq e^{-c_{\Sigma}n}
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Fix two vectors
w_{0}^{1},w_{0}^{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\underline{\Delta}\in\mathbb{R}
a constant satisfying
|\underline{\Delta}|\leq 8\sqrt{n}
. We can choose
c_{\Sigma}>0
small enough and choose
L>1
large enough depending only on
B
so that for each
j\in[j_{0}]
and
\epsilon_{1}\geq e^{-c_{*}n/2}
, the following two estimates hold:

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{K}}

\displaystyle(\exists(v_{1},v_{2})\in\Sigma_{2^{j}\eta,\epsilon_{1}}(c):\|(A_{n}-s_{1}I_{n})v_{1}-w_{0}^{1}\|_{2}\leq 2^{-n/3},

\displaystyle\|(A_{n}-s_{2}I_{n})v_{2}-\underline{\Delta}v_{1}-w_{0}^{2}\|_{2}\leq 2^{-n/3})\leq 2^{-700n},

(4.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{K}}(\exists(v_{1},v_{2})\in\Sigma_{2^{j}\eta,0}(c):\|(A_{n}-s_{1}I_{n})v_{1}-w_{0}^{1}\|_{2}\leq 2^{-n/3},

\displaystyle\|(A_{n}-s_{2}I_{n})v_{2}-\underline{\Delta}v_{1}-w_{0}^{2}\|_{2}\leq 2^{-n/3})\leq 2^{-700n},

for any fixed values of
\epsilon_{1},c,s_{1},s_{2}
in the range specified in Corollary
4.3
.

Proof.

We begin with the first claim and again write
\epsilon=2^{j}\eta
. We first assume that
\epsilon/\epsilon_{1}
satisfy the bound (
3.15
). Then by Proposition
3.14
, for
(v_{1},v_{2})\in\Sigma_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)
we can find
(\underline{v}_{1},\underline{v}_{2})\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)
such that
\|v_{i}-\underline{v}_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq 12\epsilon n^{-1/2},i=1,2
. Then using
\|A_{n}\|_{op}\leq 4\sqrt{n}
, we get that whenever
c_{\Sigma}>0
is not too large,

(4.4)

\displaystyle\{\|(A_{n}-s_{1}I_{n})v_{1}-w_{0}^{1}\|_{2}\leq 2^{-n/3},\|(A_{n}-s_{2}I_{n})v_{2}-\underline{\Delta}v_{1}-w_{0}^{2}\|_{2}\leq 2^{-n/3}\}

\displaystyle\subset\{\|(A_{n}-s_{1}I_{n})\underline{v}_{1}-w_{0}^{1}\|_{2}\leq 300\epsilon\sqrt{n},\|(A_{n}-s_{2}I_{n})\underline{v}_{2}-\underline{\Delta}\cdot\underline{v}_{1}-w_{0}^{2}\|_{2}\leq 300\epsilon\sqrt{n}\}.

By properties of the net
\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)
and using Fact
3.16
to change
\epsilon
to
300\epsilon
, we have that

\sup_{(\underline{v}_{1},\underline{v}_{2})\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)}\mathbb{P}_{A}^{\mathcal{K}}(\|((A_{n}-s_{1}I_{n})\underline{v}_{1}-w_{0}^{1},(A_{n}-s_{2}I_{n})\underline{v}_{2}-\underline{\Delta}\cdot\underline{v}_{1}-w_{0}^{2})\|_{2}\leq 300\epsilon\sqrt{n})\leq(2^{60}\frac{L^{2}\epsilon^{2}}{\epsilon_{1}})^{n}.

By Theorem
3.8
, we have, whenever
c_{\Sigma}
is not too large, the bound for
|\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)|
:

|\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)|\leq(\frac{C_{B}}{c_{0}^{16}L^{4}\epsilon^{2}/\epsilon_{1}})^{n},

Then the probability (
4.2
) is upper bounded by

(\frac{C_{B}}{c_{0}^{16}L^{4}\epsilon^{2}/\epsilon_{1}})^{n}(2^{60}L^{2}\epsilon^{2}/\epsilon_{1})^{n}\leq 2^{-700n},

whenever we take
L>0
sufficiently large. This holds for all
j\in[j_{0}]
, so that taking a union bound over
j\in[j_{0}]
and summing the probability over
j
completes the proof.

When the assumption in (
3.15
) is not satisfied, we instead apply Proposition
3.15
, which yields that for
(v_{1},v_{2})\in\Sigma_{\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}}(c)
we can find
(\underline{v}_{1},\underline{v}_{2})\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon_{1},0}(c)
such that
\|v_{i}-\underline{v}_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq 12\epsilon_{1}n^{-1/2},i=1,2
. Then using
\|A_{n}\|_{op}\leq 4\sqrt{n}
, we get that whenever
c_{\Sigma}>0
is small enough, the inclusion (
4.4
) still holds but with
\epsilon_{1}
in place of
\epsilon
in the second line of (
4.4
).

By properties of the net
\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon_{1},0}(c)
and using Fact
3.16
to change
\epsilon
to
300\epsilon
, we have that

\sup_{(\underline{v}_{1},\underline{v}_{2})\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon_{1},0}(c)}\mathbb{P}_{A}^{\mathcal{K}}(\|((A_{n}-s_{1}I_{n})\underline{v}_{1}-w_{0}^{1},(A_{n}-s_{2}I_{n})\underline{v}_{2}-\underline{\Delta}\underline{v}_{1}-w_{0}^{2})\|_{2}\leq 300\epsilon_{1}\sqrt{n})\leq(2^{60}L^{2}\epsilon_{1})^{n}.

By Proposition
3.9
we have the following upper bound for
|\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon_{1},0}(c)|
:

|\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon_{1},0}(c)|\leq(\frac{C_{B}}{c_{0}^{22}L^{4}\epsilon_{1}})^{n}.

Then the probability (
4.2
) in this case (when (
3.15
) fails) is upper bounded by

(\frac{C_{B}}{c_{0}^{22}L^{4}\epsilon_{1}})^{n}(2^{60}L^{2}\epsilon_{1})^{n}\leq 2^{-700n},

whenever we take
L>0
sufficiently large.

The proof for (
4.3
) is very similar to the proof of the previous (second) case here, and is thus omitted.

∎

We can now reduce the proof of Theorem
2.6
to the following version:

Corollary 4.6
.

The proof of Theorem
2.6
can be reduced to the following estimate: for a given constant
c_{\Sigma}>0
and a (large, already fixed)
L>1
,
there exist
\alpha^{\prime},\gamma,\mu\in(0,1)
and
c_{\Sigma}^{\prime}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\epsilon_{1},c,I,C_{I}^{1},C_{I}^{3},s_{1},s_{2}}\sup_{w_{0}^{1},w_{0}^{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{K}}(\exists(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{1}}(c)\cup\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon_{1}}^{0}(c):D_{\alpha^{\prime},\gamma}((C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3})_{I})\leq e^{c_{\Sigma}^{\prime}n},

\displaystyle\tau_{L,\underline{C}_{I}^{3}}(v_{1},\underline{C}_{I}^{1}v_{1}+\underline{C}_{I}^{3}v_{3})\leq e^{-c_{\Sigma}n}\text{ if }|\underline{C}_{I}^{3}|\geq e^{-c_{\Sigma}n},\quad\tau_{L,0}(v_{1},\underline{C}_{I}^{1}v_{1}+\underline{C}_{I}^{3}v_{3}\left.\right)\leq e^{-c_{\Sigma}n}\text{else},

\displaystyle\|(A_{n}-s_{1}I_{n})v_{1}-w_{0}^{1}\|_{2}\leq 2^{-n/3},\quad

\displaystyle\|(A_{n}-s_{2}I_{n})(\underline{C}_{I}^{1}v_{1}+\underline{C}_{I}^{3}v_{3})+(s_{2}-s_{1})\underline{C}_{I}^{1}v_{1}-w_{0}^{2}\|_{2}\leq 2^{-n/3})\leq 2^{-600n},

where the supremum is over parameters of the same range as in Corollary
4.3
and
4.4
.

Proof.

This follows from combining Corollary
4.4
with Proposition
4.5
, where we use the latter result to rule out vectors whose threshold function is larger than
e^{-c_{\Sigma}n}
by a dyadic decomposition. We also take
L>1
large enough with respect to other constants, so that the value of the threshold function
\tau_{L,\epsilon_{1}}(v,w)
and
\tau_{L,0}(v,w)
has value smaller than
\kappa_{0}/8
for any vector pair
(v,w)
.
∎

The final step is to find a net for vectors
\underline{C}_{I}^{1}v_{1}+\underline{C}_{I}^{3}v_{3}
with essential LCD at most
e^{c_{\Sigma}^{\prime}n}
. Recall that by Fact
4.1
and its proof, we always have
(C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3})_{I}
is
(\delta^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
-incompressible for some
\delta^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}\in(0,1)
. Then by
[
29
]
or
[
5
]
, Fact III.6 we can choose
\gamma^{\prime}>0
small enough so that for any
\alpha^{\prime}>0
,
D_{\alpha^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}((C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3})_{I})\geq(2\kappa_{6})^{-1}\sqrt{n}
for some
\kappa_{6}>0
.

We then construct a net for vectors with intermediate LCD:

Fact 4.7
.

For any
\alpha^{\prime}>0
and
\epsilon\leq Kn^{-1/2}
there exists an
160\epsilon\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}
- net
G_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
for those vectors
C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3}
where
D_{\alpha^{\prime},\gamma}((C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3})_{I})\in[(4\epsilon)^{-1},(2\epsilon)^{-1}]
with cardinality

(4.5)

|G_{\epsilon}^{\prime}|\leq(\frac{192K/\kappa_{4}}{(\alpha^{\prime})^{\mu}\epsilon\sqrt{n}})^{n}\cdot\frac{3}{\kappa_{4}\epsilon}.

Proof.

By assumption (
4.1
), we have that
\frac{1}{3}\leq\|C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3}\|_{2}\leq 3/\kappa_{4}
. We find an
\epsilon
-net
\mathcal{N}_{z}
for
[\frac{1}{3},3/\kappa_{4}]
. Then consider the following subset

G_{\epsilon}:=\{\frac{zp}{\|p\|_{2}}:z\in\mathcal{N}_{z},p\in(\mathbb{Z}^{I}\oplus\sqrt{\alpha}\mathbb{Z}^{I^{c}})\cap B_{n}(0,6/(\epsilon\kappa_{4}))\setminus\{0\}\}.

By definition of essential LCD, let
D=D_{\alpha^{\prime},\gamma}((C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3})_{I})
, then we can find
p_{I}\in\mathbb{Z}^{I}\cap B_{n}(0,3/(\epsilon\kappa_{4}))
with
\|D(C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}V_{3})_{I}-p_{I}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}
and
p_{I}\neq 0
. We can also find
p_{I^{c}}\in\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathbb{Z}^{I^{c}}\cap B_{n}(0,3/(\epsilon\kappa_{4}))
with
\|D(C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}V_{3})_{I^{c}}-p_{I^{c}}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}
. Taking
p=p_{I}\oplus p_{I^{c}}
and taking
z\in\mathcal{N}_{1}
be such that
|\|C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3}\|_{2}-z|\leq\epsilon
, then by triangle inequality we have
|Dz-\|p\|_{2}|\leq 2\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}+1
. Then by triangle inequality again, we have

\displaystyle\|C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3}-\frac{zp}{\|p\|_{2}}\|_{2}\leq\|C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3}-\frac{p}{D}\|_{2}+\|\frac{p}{D}-\frac{zp}{\|p\|_{2}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2D^{-1}\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}+\|\frac{p}{D}-\frac{zp}{\|p\|_{2}}\|_{2}\leq 5D^{-1}\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}\leq 80\epsilon\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}.

By a volumetric argument (see
[
29
]
), the cardinality of
G_{\epsilon}
is bounded by
(\frac{192K\kappa_{4}}{(\alpha^{\prime})^{\mu}\epsilon\sqrt{n}})^{n}\cdot\frac{3}{\kappa_{4}\epsilon}
. Although this subset
G_{\epsilon}
is not a net of
C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3}
, it can easily be modified to be a
160\epsilon\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}
- net
G_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
of
C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3}
of the same cardinality. This completes the proof.
∎

Now we can complete the proof of Theorem
2.6
.

Proof of Theorem

2.6
.

We only need to justify the claimed estimates in Corollary
4.6
.
Consider the first case in Corollary
4.6
where
|C_{I}^{3}|\geq e^{-c_{\Sigma}n}
.
We take a dyadic decomposition for the possible range of essential LCD and consider each level set of LCD value

\cup_{j=1}^{j_{1}}\Sigma_{\alpha^{\prime},\gamma}(j),\quad\Sigma_{\alpha^{\prime},\gamma}(j):=\{(v_{1},v_{3}):{D_{\alpha^{\prime},\gamma}((C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3})_{I})\in[2^{j}\eta_{0},2^{j+1}\eta_{0}]\}},

with
\eta_{0}=(2\kappa_{6})^{-1}\sqrt{n}
and
j_{1}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
satisfies
2^{j_{1}}\eta_{0}\geq e^{c_{\Sigma}^{\prime}n}\geq 2^{j_{1}-1}\eta_{0}
. We fix a
j\in[j_{1}]
and let
\epsilon_{j}
satisfy
(2\epsilon_{j})^{-1}=2^{j+1}\eta_{0}
. By Fact
4.7
, the subset
\Sigma_{\alpha^{\prime},\gamma}(j)
has a
160\epsilon\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}
-net
G_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime}
of cardinality bounded by
(\frac{192K/\kappa_{4}}{(\alpha^{\prime})^{\mu}\epsilon_{j}\sqrt{n}})^{n}\cdot\frac{3}{\kappa_{4}\epsilon_{j}}.

Now we consider two possible cases: (I) when
|C_{I}^{1}|\geq 0.5
and (II) when
|C_{I}^{1}|\leq 0.5
but
|C_{I}^{3}|\geq 0.5
. By assumption (
4.1
), we are in at least one of the two cases. In case (I), we can find a trivial
\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}\epsilon_{j}
- net for
C_{I}^{3}v_{3}
of cardinality bounded by
(\frac{100K/\kappa_{4}}{(\epsilon_{j}/C_{I}^{3})\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}})^{n}
which we denote by
G_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime\prime}
, and it is not hard to check using
|C_{I}^{1}|\geq 0.5
that the product of these two nets can yield a
1000\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}\epsilon_{j}
-net for
v_{1}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
: first note that we can find
w_{1}\in G_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime}
,
w_{2}\in G_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime\prime}
such that

\|C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3}-w_{1}\|_{2}\leq 160\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}\epsilon_{j},\quad\|C_{I}^{3}v_{3}-w_{2}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}\epsilon_{j},

then by triangle inequality

\|v_{1}-(w_{1}-w_{2})/(C_{I}^{1})\|_{2}\leq 400\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}\epsilon_{j},

and by a standard argument, we can modify the product of the nets
G_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime}\times G_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime\prime}
to be a
1000\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}\epsilon_{j}
- net
\mathcal{N}_{(I)}^{j}
for
(v_{1},C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3})
of cardinality at most
|G_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime}|\cdot|G_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime\prime}|
.

Similarly in case (II) which is indeed much simpler, we also use a trivial
\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}\epsilon_{j}
-net
G_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
for
v_{1}
and we can check using
|C_{I}^{3}|\geq 0.5
that the product of these two nets can yield a
1000\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}\epsilon_{j}
-net
\mathcal{N}_{(II)}^{j}
for
(v_{1},C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3})
with cardinality bounded by
|G_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime}|\cdot|G_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\prime\prime\prime}|
.

Then in case (I), for
(v_{1},v_{3})\in\Sigma_{\alpha^{\prime},\gamma}(j)
, we take
(\hat{v}_{1},C_{I}^{1}\hat{v}_{1}+C_{I}^{3}\hat{v}_{3})\in\mathcal{N}_{(I)}^{j}
approximating
v_{1},C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3}
, then on the event
\|A_{n}\|_{op}\leq 4\sqrt{n}
(and for
c_{\Sigma}^{\prime}>0
small enough),

\displaystyle\{\|(A-s_{1})v_{1}-w_{0}^{1}\|_{2},\|(A-s_{2})(\underline{C}_{I}^{1}v_{1}+\underline{C}_{I}^{3}v_{3})+(s_{2}-s_{1})\underline{C}_{I}^{1}v_{1}-w_{0}^{2}\|_{2}\leq 2^{-n/3})\}\subseteq

\displaystyle\{\|(A-s_{1})\hat{v}_{1}-w_{0}^{1}\|_{2},\|(A-s_{2})(\underline{C}_{I}^{1}\hat{v}_{1}+\underline{C}_{I}^{3}\hat{v}_{3})+(s_{2}-s_{1})\underline{C}_{I}^{1}\hat{v}_{1}-w_{0}^{2}\|_{2}\leq 5000\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n^{2}}\epsilon_{j}\},

so that the probability in question for Corollary
4.6
for vectors in the
j
-th level set of LCD can be upper bounded by

(4.6)

(\frac{192K/\kappa_{4}}{(\alpha^{\prime})^{\mu}\epsilon_{j}\sqrt{n}})^{n}\cdot(\frac{100K/\kappa_{4}}{(\epsilon_{j}/C_{I}^{3})\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}})^{n}\cdot\frac{3}{\kappa_{4}\epsilon_{j}}\cdot(4L^{2}10^{8}\alpha^{\prime}n\epsilon_{j}^{2}/C_{I}^{3})^{n}\leq 2^{-700n},

where the first three terms is the cardinality of the net
\mathcal{N}_{(I)}^{j}
, and the fourth term arises from the threshold function being small:
\tau_{L,\underline{C}_{I}^{3}}(\hat{v}_{1},\underline{C}_{I}^{1}\hat{v}_{1}+\underline{C}_{I}^{3}\hat{v}_{3})\leq e^{-c_{\Sigma}n}
(those
(\hat{v}_{1},\hat{v}_{3})
not satisfying this are ruled out in Corollary
4.6
).
The final inequality follows from taking
\alpha^{\prime}>0
small enough.
For this chosen
\alpha^{\prime}
, we take
c_{\Sigma}^{\prime}>0
to be small enough so that
\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}\exp({-c_{\Sigma}^{\prime}n})\geq\exp(-c_{\Sigma}n)
, allowing us to use the information of the threshold function at (
4.6
). The case in (II) is similar. Taking the summation over
j
completes the proof.

For the remaining case where
|C_{I}^{3}|\leq e^{-c_{\Sigma}n}
, an
\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}\epsilon_{j}
-net for
C_{I}^{1}v_{1}+C_{I}^{3}v_{3}
yields a
2\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}\epsilon_{j}
net for
C_{I}^{1}v_{1}
when
\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}\epsilon_{j}\geq e^{-c_{\Sigma}n}
( choose
c_{\Sigma}^{\prime}
as previously, so this holds for all
j\in[j_{1}]
), which yields a
4\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}n}\epsilon_{j}
-net for
v_{1}
because in this case
|C_{I}^{1}|\geq 0.5
. No additional nets need to be constructed for the
C_{I}^{3}v_{3}
component. Then we use the information on the threshold function
\tau_{L,0}(v_{1},\underline{C}_{I}^{1}v_{1}+\underline{C}_{I}^{3}v_{3})
to complete the proof similarly as in the first case.

∎

5.
Reducing singular value to distance estimates

The aim of this section is to reduce the lower bound estimate on two singular values to a random distance problem. This is a typical step in the invertibility via distance approach in
[
29
]
. For symmetric random matrices, the reduction is more difficult and even more so when we handle two locations. The main result of the section is the following:

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
A_{n+1}\sim\operatorname{Sym}_{n+1}(\zeta)
,
X\sim\operatorname{Col}_{n}(\zeta)
and
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. For any
p>1
and
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}>0
, we have the following estimate:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}-\lambda_{i}I_{n+1})\leq\delta_{i}n^{-1/2},i=1,2)\lesssim\delta_{1}\delta_{2}+e^{-\Omega(n)}

\displaystyle+\sup_{r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{P}_{A_{n},X}\left(\frac{|\langle(A_{n}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})^{-1}X,X\rangle-r_{i}|}{\|(A_{n}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})^{-1}X\|_{2}}\leq\delta_{i},i=1,2,\frac{\mu_{1}(\lambda_{1})\mu_{1}(\lambda_{2})}{n}\leq(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{-p}\right),

where we denote
\mu_{1}(\lambda_{i}):=\sigma_{max}((A_{n}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})^{-1})
for both
i=1,2
.

Remark 5.2
.

To prove Theorem
1.2
, we can assume that
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\in[e^{-c_{*}n/2},1]
for any given
c_{*}>0
. If any one of the
\delta_{i}
is larger than 1 then we are reduced to the one-location estimate (see Proposition
2.9
). Otherwise, if one of them is less than
e^{-c_{*}n/2}
, then again by the one-location estimate, Proposition
2.9
, the resulting probability estimate is exponentially small and thus can be squeezed in the
e^{-\Omega(n)}
error term.

We shall also always assume
A_{n}-\lambda_{i}I_{n}
is invertible: this can be achieved by adding a very small Gaussian noise to
A_{n}
which does not change the resulting estimates.

The fact that in the stated estimate we rule out the event
\{\mu_{1}(\lambda_{1})\mu_{1}(\lambda_{2})\geq n(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{-p}\}
is because our estimates in the forthcoming chapters will deteriorate on this event. Rather, the estimate on this event can be worked out fairly easily via a separate argument in Proposition
5.3
. This can be compared to the one-location case in
[
5
]
, Lemma 6.1. In our two-location estimate, the event we take away depends on the product of
\mu_{1}(\lambda_{1})
and
\mu_{1}(\lambda_{2})
, and also on a constant
p>1
that will be set arbitrarily close to 1.

We first consider the ruled-out event
\{\mu_{1}(\lambda_{1})\mu_{1}(\lambda_{2})\geq n(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{-p}\}
.
For this purpose, we define for any
p\geq 1
,

\mathcal{P}^{p}:=\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{2}\sigma_{min}(A^{(j)}_{n+1}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})\leq(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{p}n^{-1}\text{ for at least }\frac{1}{4}n\text{ values }j\in[n+1]\right\},

where
A_{n+1}^{(j)}
is the principal submatrix of
A_{n+1}
with the
j
-th row and column removed.

The estimate on the event
\mathcal{P}^{p}
is easier to handle, as shown in the following:

Proposition 5.3
.

Fix
B>0
,
\zeta\in\Gamma_{B}
, and take
A_{n+1}\sim\operatorname{Sym}_{n+1}(\zeta)
. Then for any
p>1
,

(5.1)

\mathbb{P}(\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}-\lambda_{i}I_{n+1})\leq\delta_{i}n^{-1/2},i=1,2,\quad\mathcal{P}^{p})\lesssim\delta_{1}\delta_{2}+e^{-\Omega(n)}.

5.1.
Proof of Proposition
5.3

We need two auxiliary results. The first concerns the eigenvector delocalization of Wigner matrices, taken from Rudelson and Vershynin
[
27
]
.

Theorem 5.4
.

(
[
27
]
) Fix
B>0
and let
\zeta\in\Gamma_{B}
. Consider
A_{n}\sim\operatorname{Sym}_{n}(\zeta)
and let
v
be any eigenvector of
A_{n}
. Then there is some
c_{2}>0
such that for any sufficiently small
c_{1}>0
, for
n
sufficiently large,

\mathbb{P}(|v_{j}|\geq(c_{2}c_{1})^{6}n^{-1/2}\text{ for at least }(1-c_{1})n\text{ indices }j)\geq 1-e^{-c_{1}n}.

We also need the following geometric fact from
[
5
]
, Fact 6.7.

Fact 5.5
.

Given any
n\times n
real symmetric matrix
M
and
\lambda
as an eigenvector of
M
with associated eigenvector
u
. Let
j\in[n]
and assume that
\lambda^{\prime}
is an eigenvector of the principal minor
M^{(j)}
corresponding to a unit eigenvector
v
. Then we have

|\langle v,X^{(j)}\rangle|\leq|\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}|/|u_{j}|,

where
X^{(j)}
denotes the
j
-th column of
M
of which the
j
-th entry is removed.

We also use a standard version of the inverse Littlewood-Offord theorem
[
29
]
.

Theorem 5.6
.

Fix
B>0
,
\gamma,\alpha\in(0,1)
and
v\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
with
D_{\alpha,\gamma}(v)\geq c\epsilon^{-1}
. Let
X\sim\operatorname{Col}_{n}(\zeta)
with
\zeta\in\Gamma_{B}
. Then

\mathbb{P}(|\langle X,v\rangle|\leq\epsilon)\lesssim\epsilon+e^{-c\alpha n}

for some
c>0
depending only on
B
and
\gamma
.

Now we can prove Proposition
5.3
. We discuss the proof heuristics first. The event
\mathcal{P}^{p}
can be (approximately, though not correctly) interpreted as claiming that both
\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}^{(j)}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})
are smaller than
\delta_{i}
,
i=1,2
. This is included in the event that an eigenvalue of
A_{n+1}
and an eigenvalue of
A_{n+1}^{(j)}
have a difference upper bounded by
2\delta_{i}
, say. Then we use Fact
5.5
and the inverse Littlewood-Offord theorem. However, our definition of
\mathcal{P}^{p}
is about the product of least singular value at two locations, so we have to discretize and consider all the possible cases of the value they may take. Also, we are considering joint estimates for two locations which are not available before, so we first use the estimate for
A_{n}^{(j)}
in one location and apply the one-point estimate (Proposition
2.9
), then use Fact
5.5
at the other location. This procedure enables us to successfully decouple the two locations.

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

Let
Q_{1}
be the event where the conclusions of Theorem
5.4
hold true, and we work on
Q_{1}
. We first find some value
j\in[n+1]
such that the event in
\mathcal{P}^{p}
holds for the subscript
j
and the unit eigenvectors (denoted
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
) of
A_{n+1}
corresponding to the least singular values of
A_{n+1}-\lambda_{1}I_{n+1}
and
A_{n+1}-\lambda_{2}I_{n+1}
respectively, satisfy
|(u_{1})_{j}|,|(u_{2})_{j}|\geq(c_{2}c_{1})^{6}n^{-1/2}
for some fixed
c_{1},c_{2}
. The fact that such
j
exists among the given
\frac{1}{4}n
values of
j
specified in
\mathcal{P}^{p}
is guaranteed by Theorem
5.4
on the event
Q_{1}
, which holds with probability
1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
.

Now we would like to find a dyadic decomposition of
[(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{p},1]=\cup_{j=1}^{I_{n}}I_{j}
into
I_{n}
intervals, where we denote by
I_{j}=[I_{j,S},I_{j,L}]
such that
I_{j,L}=2I_{j,S}
and
I_{j,S}=I_{j+1,L}
(except for
I_{1}
, where we do slightly differently). Then we have
I_{n}=O(\log(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{-1})
and we can decompose the event
\{\prod_{i=1}^{2}\sigma_{min}(A_{n}^{(j)}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})\leq(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{p}n^{-1}\}
as a union of
(I_{n})^{2}
events, where each event has the form
\{\sigma_{min}(A_{n}^{(j)}-\lambda_{1}I_{n})\leq x_{1}n^{-1/2}
,
\sigma_{min}(A_{n}^{(j)}-\lambda_{2}I_{n})\leq x_{2}n^{-1/2}\}
for two real numbers
x_{1},x_{2}>0
satisfying that
x_{1}x_{2}\leq 4(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{p}
. Note that if for some
i\in\{1,2\}
we already have
\sigma_{min}(A_{n}^{(j)}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})\leq(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{p}n^{-1/2}
we may directly apply the one- location estimate, Proposition
2.9
to
A_{n}-\lambda_{i}I_{n}
and the contribution of this case to the probability on the left hand side of (
5.1
) is at most

\mathbb{P}(\sigma_{min}(A_{n}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})\leq(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{p}n^{-1.2})\lesssim(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{p}+e^{-cn},

and then use the fact
(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{p}\leq\delta_{1}\delta_{2}
. Or, if for some
i
we have
\sigma_{min}(A_{n}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})\geq n^{-1/2}
then for the other
\ell\neq i
we must have
\sigma_{min}(A_{n}-\lambda_{\ell}I_{n})\leq(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{p}n^{-1/2}
, and we are reduced to the first case. Thus we can always assume that
x_{1}x_{2}\leq 4(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{p}
and that
x_{1},x_{2}\in[(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{p},1]
.

Now we analyze each of these
(I_{n})^{2}
events
\{\sigma_{min}(A_{n}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})\leq x_{i}n^{-1/2},i=1,2\}
.

There are two scenarios. We first assume that for at least one
i\in\{1,2\}
we have
x_{i}\geq\delta_{i}
. Let us take
i=1
without loss of generality, that is,
\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}^{(j)}-\lambda_{1}I_{n})\leq x_{1}
. We first consider the event on location
\lambda_{2}
:
\{\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}^{(j)}-\lambda_{2}I_{n})\leq x_{2}n^{-1/2}
}. By Proposition
2.9
, we have the bound

(5.2)

\mathbb{P}\left(\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}^{(j)}-\lambda_{2}I_{n})\leq x_{2}n^{-1/2}\right)\lesssim x_{2}+e^{-\Omega(n)}.

Now we condition on this event and estimate the singular value at location
\lambda_{1}
. By assumption that
x_{1}\geq\delta_{1}
, we need only to bound

(5.3)

\mathbb{P}\left(\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}-\lambda_{1}I_{n+1})\leq x_{1}n^{-1/2},\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}^{(j)}-\lambda_{1}I_{n})\leq x_{1}n^{-1/2}\mid\mathcal{E}_{2,p}\right)

where we are conditioning on the event

\mathcal{E}_{2,p}:=\{\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}^{(j)}-\lambda_{2}I_{n})\leq x_{2}n^{-1/2}\}.

To bound the probability of this event, let
v^{j}
be the unit eigenvector associated with the least singular value of
A_{n+1}^{(j)}-\lambda_{1}I_{n}
. Let
Q_{3}
denote the event
Q_{3}:=\{D_{\alpha,\gamma}(v^{j})\geq e^{c_{3}n}\text{ for all }j\in[n+1]\}
, where we have
\mathbb{P}(Q_{3}^{c})\leq e^{-\Omega(n)}
by Lemma

2.1
.
Now we can use Fact
5.5
since both
\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}-\lambda_{1}I_{n+1})
and
\sigma_{min}(A_{n}^{(j)}-\lambda_{1}I_{n})
are smaller than
x_{1}n^{-1/2}
, and we also use the fact that the index
j\in[n+1]
is chosen at the beginning of the proof such that
|(u_{1})_{j}|\geq(c_{1}c_{2})^{6}n^{-1/2}
.

Then we conclude by the inverse Littlewood-Offord theorem, Theorem
5.6
that

(5.4)

\eqref{conditionone}\leq\mathbb{P}(|\langle X^{(j)},v\rangle|\leq 2(c_{1}c_{2})^{-6}x_{1})+\mathbb{P}(Q_{1}^{c}\cup Q_{3}^{c})\lesssim x_{1}+e^{-\Omega(n)}.

Then we can combine this with (
5.2
) to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}-\lambda_{i}I_{n+1})\leq\delta_{i}n^{-1/2},\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}^{(j)}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})\leq x_{i}n^{1/2},i=1,.2\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim x_{1}x_{2}+e^{-\Omega(n)}\lesssim(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{p}+e^{-\Omega(n)}.

Then we are left with the remaining case where
x_{1}<\delta_{1}
and
x_{2}<\delta_{2}
. Our assumption that
x_{1}x_{2}<(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{p}
implies that for some
i=1,2
we have
x_{i}\leq\delta_{i}(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{(p-1)/2}
. Let us say we have
i=2
(
i=1
is analogous), so
x_{2}\leq\delta_{2}(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{(p-1)/2}
.

We first apply Proposition
2.9
and obtain the same estimate as in (
5.2
). We then apply fact
5.5
and, on the event
Q_{3}
, apply Theorem
5.6
to obtain

(5.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}-\lambda_{1}I_{n+1})\leq\delta_{1}n^{-1/2},\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}^{(j)}-\lambda_{1}I_{n})\leq\delta_{1}n^{-1/2}\mid\mathcal{E}_{2,p}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta_{1}+e^{-\Omega(n)}.

Combining these two estimates, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}-\lambda_{i}I_{n+1})\leq\delta_{i}n^{-1/2},\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}^{(j)}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})\leq x_{i}n^{1/2},i=1,.2\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta_{1}x_{2}+e^{-\Omega(n)}\lesssim(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{(p+1)/2}+e^{-\Omega(n)}.

Finally, we take a union bound over all all the
(I_{n})^{2}
events (i.e. choices of
x_{1},x_{2})
, which leads to an entropy cost of
\log((\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{-1})^{C_{d}}\lesssim(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{1-p}
. This completes the proof.
∎

5.2.
Proof of of Proposition
5.1

Now we have shown that in order to prove

\mathbb{P}(\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}-\lambda_{i}I_{n+1})\leq\delta_{i}n^{-1/2},i=1,2)\lesssim\delta_{1}\delta_{2}+e^{-\Omega(n)},

it suffices to prove, for any
p>1
,

(5.6)

\mathbb{P}(\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}-\lambda_{i}I_{n+1})\leq\delta_{i}n^{-1/2},i=1,2,\quad\wedge\quad(\mathcal{P}^{p})^{c})\lesssim\delta_{1}\delta_{2}+e^{-\Omega(n)}.

First, we recall a fundamental fact (see
[
29
]
):

Fact 5.7
.

For any
n\times n
symmetric matrix, let
v
be the unit vector corresponding to the least singular value of
M
, that is,
\|Mv\|_{2}=\sigma_{min}(M)
and
\|v\|_{2}=1
. Then we have

\sigma_{min}(M)\geq|v_{j}|\cdot d_{j}(M)\quad\text{ for each }j\in[n],

where
d_{j}(M)
is the distance of the
j
-th column of
M
to the subspace spanned by the other
n-1
columns of
M
.

The distance
d_{j}(M)
can be computed as follows: (see
[
34
]
, Prop.5.1)

Fact 5.8
.

Let
A_{n+1}
be an
n+1\times n+1
symmetric matrix where
A_{n}
is its principal submatrix with the first row/ column of
A_{n+1}
removed. Let
a_{11}
be the entry of
A_{n+1}
at location (1,1) and
X
the first row of
A_{n+1}
with the fist entry removed. Then

d_{1}(A_{n+1})=\frac{|\langle A^{-1}X,X\rangle-a_{11}|}{\sqrt{1+\|A^{-1}X\|_{2}^{2}}}.

The following lower bound for
\|A^{-1}X\|_{2}
is available: (see
[
34
]
, Prop.8.2)

Fact 5.9
.

Let
B>0
,
\zeta\in\Gamma_{B}
and
A_{n+1}\sim\operatorname{Sym}_{n+1}(\zeta)
. Fix any
\lambda\in[-4\sqrt{n},4\sqrt{n}]
. Then with probability at least
1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
, we have

\|(A_{n}-\lambda I_{n})^{-1}X\|_{2}\geq\frac{1}{15}.

Now we are in the place to prove the main result, Proposition
5.1
. The proof uses a standard first moment method to express smallness of singular value by smallness of certain random distance functions. The crucial new input here is that we have two different locations
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
, and we need to guarantee that their least singular vectors have a large enough overlapping support. This is guaranteed by the no-gaps delocalization result, Theorem
5.4
.

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

Let the eigenvectors associated with the least singular value of
A_{n+1}-\lambda_{i}I_{n+1}
be denoted by
u_{i}
for each
i=1,2
. Let
Q_{1}^{c}
be the rare event that, for some
i=1,2
,
u_{i}
does not satisfy the conclusion of Theorem
5.4
where we take
c_{1}=\frac{1}{4}
. Then
\mathbb{P}(Q_{1}^{c})=e^{-\Omega(n)}
. On
Q_{1}
, we have that for at least
\frac{1}{2}n
choices of
j\in[n+1]
,

(5.7)

\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}-\lambda_{i}I_{n+1})\geq c_{3}n^{-1/2}\cdot d_{j}(A_{n+1}-\lambda_{i}I_{n+1})\text{ for both }i=1,2,

where
c_{3}>0
depends only on
B
.

Furthermore, taking the intersection of these
\frac{1}{2}n
values of
j
with the
\frac{3}{4}n
values of
j
where the claim in
(\mathcal{P}^{p})^{c}
does not hold, we get that on the event
(\mathcal{P}^{p})^{c}
, there are at least
\frac{1}{4}n
values of
j\in[n+1]
such that (
5.7
) holds for such
j
and moreover,

\prod_{i=1}^{2}\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}^{(j)}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})\geq(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{p}n^{-1}.

Now let
S\subset\{1,2,\cdots,n+1\}
denote the collection of subscripts
j\in[n+1]
such that the following event
\mathcal{H}_{j}
holds (for some universal constant
C
)

\mathcal{H}_{j}:=\left\{d_{j}(A_{n+1}-\lambda_{i}I_{n+1})\leq C\delta_{i}\text{ for }i=1,2,\prod_{i=1}^{2}\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}^{(j)}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})\geq(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{p}n^{-1}\right\}.

Our previous discussions imply that

\left\{\sigma_{min}(A_{n+1}-\lambda_{i}I_{n+1})\leq\delta_{i}n^{-1/2},i=1,2\right\}\bigcap(\mathcal{P}^{p})^{c}\bigcap Q_{1}\Rightarrow|S|>\frac{1}{4}n.

By a standard first moment estimate,

\mathbb{P}(|S|>\frac{1}{4}n)\leq\frac{4}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{H}_{j}).

Thus there exists some
j_{*}\in[n+1]
such that
\mathbb{P}(|S|\geq\frac{1}{4}n)\leq 5\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{H}_{j_{*}})
, and we can take
j_{*}=1
without loss of generality.

In the final step, we use Fact
5.8
to expand the distance
d_{j}(A_{n+1}-\lambda_{i}I_{n+1})
and use the estimate in Fact
5.9
to complete the proof.
∎

6.
Decoupling of the quadratic forms

The main result of this section is to prove the following decoupling lemma.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\zeta\in\Gamma_{B}
for some
B>0
. Let
A_{n}
be any
n\times n
symmetric matrix with
A_{n}\in\mathcal{E}
(see Lemma
2.1
for the event
\mathcal{E}
, and let
c_{*}>0
,
\mu>0
be the two constants given there) and let
X\sim\operatorname{Col}_{n}(\zeta)
be a random vector independent of
A_{n}
. Consider two fixed locations
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in[-4\sqrt{n},4\sqrt{n}]\subset\mathbb{R}
.

For each
i=1,2
denote by
\mu_{1}(\lambda_{i}):=\sigma_{\operatorname{max}}\left((A_{n}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})^{-1}\right)
. Then, for any
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and any
u\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
, we have the estimate for any
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\in[e^{-c_{*}n/2},1]:

(6.1)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{X}\left(\left|\left\langle(A_{n}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})^{-1}X,X\rangle-t_{i}\right|\leq\delta_{i}\mu_{1}(\lambda_{i}),\quad i=1,2;\quad\langle X,u\right\rangle\geq s\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta_{1}\delta_{2}\cdot e^{-s}\cdot\int_{\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\sum_{i=1}^{2}|\theta_{i}\delta_{i}|^{2}\leq 2\}}I(\theta)^{\frac{1}{2}}d\theta+e^{-\Omega(n)},

where
I(\theta)
is defined as follows:

(6.2)

I(\theta):=\mathbb{E}_{J,X_{J},X_{J}^{\prime}}\exp\left(\langle(X+X^{\prime})_{J},u\rangle-c\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{\theta_{i}}{\mu_{1}(\lambda_{i})}(A_{n}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})^{-1}(X-X^{\prime})_{J}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right),

where
X^{\prime}\sim\operatorname{Col}_{n}(\zeta)
is independent of
X
and we take
J\subseteq[n]
to be a
\mu
-random set (i.e., a subset where each element in
[n]
has (indepndently) probability
\mu
to belong to this subset).

Note that, compared to the expression in Proposition
5.1
, we have replaced the term
\|(A_{n}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})^{-1}X\|_{2}
in the denominator there by the minimal singular value
\mu_{1}(\lambda_{i})
here, which is a lot more controllable. We will turn back to
\|A_{n}^{-1}X\|_{2}
much later, in Section
10
.

To prove this theorem, we need a number of preparatory results. First, we need an Esseen-type inequality with an additional decoupling of different types of constraints:

Lemma 6.2
.

For
B>0
consider
\zeta\in\Gamma_{B}
and
X\sim\operatorname{Col}_{n}(\zeta)
. Let
M_{1},M_{2}
be two
n\times n
symmetric matrices,
u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
s,\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\geq 0
. Then

(6.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(|\langle M_{i}X,X\rangle-t_{i}|<\delta_{i},i=1,2,\langle X,u\rangle\geq s)

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta_{1}\delta_{2}e^{-s}\int_{\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\theta_{1}^{2}\delta_{1}^{2}+\theta_{2}^{2}\delta_{2}^{2}\leq 2\}}|\mathbb{E}e^{2\pi i\theta_{1}\langle M_{1}X,X\rangle+2\pi i\theta_{2}\langle M_{2}X,X\rangle+\langle X,u\rangle}|d\theta.

Proof.

The proof consists of two parts: (i) removing the soft constraint
\langle X,u\rangle\geq s
and (ii) applying Esséen’s lemma. First we can bound, for any
s>0
,

(6.4)

\mathbb{P}(|\langle M_{i}X,X\rangle-t_{i}|\leq\delta_{i},i=1,2,\langle X,u\rangle\geq s)\leq e^{-s}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}(|\langle M_{i}X,X\rangle-t_{i}|\leq\delta_{i})e^{\langle X,u\rangle}].

Then define a random variable
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\mathbb{P}(Y\in U)=(\mathbb{E}e^{\langle X,u\rangle})^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{X}[1_{\mathbf{U}}e^{\langle X,u\rangle}],

so that

(6.5)

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}(|\langle M_{i}X,X\rangle-t_{i}|\leq\delta_{i})e^{\langle X,u\rangle}]=\mathbb{E}e^{\langle X,u\rangle}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}(|\langle M_{i}Y,Y\rangle-t_{i}|\leq\delta_{i},i=1,2)].

Then we shall use the following version of multidimensional Esséen’s lemma (see
[
14
]
): let
Z
be a random vector in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, then

\sup_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}\mathbb{P}(\|Z-v\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{m})\leq C^{m}\int_{B(0,\sqrt{m})}|\phi_{Z}(\theta)|d\theta,

with
\phi_{Z}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}\exp(2\pi i\langle\theta,Z\rangle)
.

In our case we take
m=2
and
Z=(\delta_{1}^{-1}\langle M_{1}Y,Y\rangle,\delta_{2}^{-1}\langle M_{2}Y,Y\rangle)
. Then we have

\mathbb{P}(|\langle M_{i}Y,Y\rangle-t_{i}|\leq\delta_{i},i=1,2)\lesssim\int_{|\theta\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{2}}[\mathbb{E}e^{2\pi i\frac{\theta_{1}}{\delta_{1}}\langle M_{1}Y,Y\rangle+2\pi i\frac{\theta_{2}}{\delta_{2}}\langle M_{2}Y,Y\rangle}]d\theta.

Then we take the change of coordinate
(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\mapsto(\delta_{1}\theta_{1},\delta_{2}\theta_{2})
and deduce that

\mathbb{P}(|\langle M_{i}Y,Y\rangle-t_{i}|\leq\delta_{i},i=1,2)\lesssim\delta_{1}\delta_{2}\int_{\{\theta_{1}^{2}\delta_{1}^{2}+\theta_{2}^{2}\delta_{2}^{2}\leq 2\}}[\mathbb{E}e^{2\pi i\theta_{1}\langle M_{1}Y,Y\rangle+2\pi i\theta_{2}\langle M_{2}Y,Y\rangle}]d\theta,

and the right hand side equals, by definition of
Y
, to

(\mathbb{E}e^{\langle X,u\rangle})^{-1}\delta_{1}\delta_{2}\int_{\{\theta_{1}^{2}\delta_{1}^{2}+\theta_{2}^{2}\delta_{2}^{2}\leq 2\}}[\mathbb{E}e^{2\pi i\theta_{1}\langle M_{1}X,X\rangle+2\pi i\theta_{2}\langle M_{2}X,X\rangle+\langle X,u\rangle}]d\theta.

Combining this with (
6.4
) and (
6.5
) completes the proof.
∎

To control the quadratic form on the right hand side of (
6.3
), we use the following lemma from
[
5
]
, Lemma 5.3:

Lemma 6.3
.

Take
\zeta\in\Gamma
,
X,X^{\prime}\sim\operatorname{Col}_{n}(\zeta)
two independent random vectors and take
[n]=I\cup J
be a partition. Let
u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
M
an
n\times n
symmetric matrix. Then we have

|\mathbb{E}_{X}e^{2\pi i\langle MX,X\rangle+\langle X,v\rangle}|^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}_{X_{J},X_{J}^{\prime}}e^{\langle(X+X^{\prime})_{J},u\rangle}\cdot\left|\mathbb{E}_{X_{I}}e^{4\pi i\langle M(X-X^{\prime})_{J},X_{I}\rangle+2\langle X_{I},u\rangle}\right|.

We then have an elementary lemma (
[
5
]
, Lemma 5.5) to estimate the exponential tilt:

Lemma 6.4
.

Take
B>0
,
\zeta\in\Gamma_{B}
and
X\sim\operatorname{Col}_{n}(\zeta)
. Then we can find some
c\in(0,1)
depending only on
B
so that for any
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

(6.6)

|\mathbb{E}_{X}e^{2\pi i\langle X,v\rangle+\langle X,u\rangle}|\leq\exp(-c\min_{r\in[1,c^{-1}]}\|rv\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{2}+c^{-1}\|u\|_{2}^{2}).

We then define a family of quasirandom events on
(J,X_{J},X_{J}^{\prime})
that are satisfied by
A_{n}\in\mathcal{E}
with very high probability. First define
\mathcal{F}_{1}
via

(6.7)

\mathcal{F}_{1}:=\{|J|\in[\mu n/2,2\mu n]\}.

We write
\widetilde{X}=X-X^{\prime}
, and then define
\mathcal{F}_{2}
via

(6.8)

\mathcal{F}_{2}:=\{\|\widetilde{X}\|_{2}n^{-1/2}\in[c,c^{-1}]\},

and define
\mathcal{F}_{3}(A_{n})
via

(6.9)

\mathcal{F}_{3}(A_{n}):=\{\forall\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{*},\|\theta\|_{2}\leq e^{c_{*}n}:[\theta\cdot A_{n}^{-1}\widetilde{X}]/\|[\theta\cdot A_{n}^{-1}\widetilde{X}]\|_{2}\in\operatorname{Incomp}(\delta,\rho)\},

where we recall that
[\theta\cdot A_{n}^{-1}\widetilde{X}]
was defined in (
2.2
).

Finally, we take
v_{1}=(A_{n}-\lambda_{1}I_{n})^{-1}\widetilde{X}
,
v_{2}=(A_{n}-\lambda_{2}I_{n})^{-1}\widetilde{X}
and
I:=[n]\setminus J
. Then define the event
\mathcal{F}_{4}(A_{n})
via

(6.10)

\mathcal{F}_{4}(A_{n}):=\{\inf_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{*},\|\theta\|_{2}\leq e^{c_{*}n}}D_{\alpha,\gamma}(\frac{(\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2})_{I}}{\|(\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2})_{I}\|_{2}})\geq e^{c_{\Sigma}n}\text{for those }\|(\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2})_{I}\|_{2}\geq 1\}.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
A_{n}\sim\operatorname{Sym}_{n}(\zeta)
with
A_{n}\in\mathcal{E}
(see Lemma
2.1
), and define
\mathcal{F}(A_{n}):=\mathcal{F}_{1}\cap\mathcal{F}_{2}\cap\mathcal{F}_{3}(A_{n})\cap\mathcal{F}_{4}(A_{n})
.
We may choose
c\in(0,1)
as a function of
B
and
\mu
so that

\mathbb{P}_{J,X_{J},X_{J}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F}^{c})\lesssim e^{-cn}.

Proof of Lemma
6.5
.

For the event
\mathcal{F}_{1}
, we use Hoeffding’s inequality to get
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{c})\leq e^{-\Omega(n)}
. For the event
\mathcal{F}_{2}
, since each coordinate of
\widetilde{X}/\sqrt{2\mu}
is i.i.d. mean 0 variance 1 and has subgaussian moment at most
B/\sqrt{2\mu}
, we can use
[
33
]
, Theorem 3.1 to show
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}_{2}^{c})\leq e^{-\Omega(n)}
for a suitable
c>0
. The probability upper bound for
\mathcal{F}_{3}(A_{n})
and
\mathcal{F}_{4}(A_{n})
follows from the fact that
A_{n}\in\mathcal{E}
and Markov’s inequality.

∎

Now we finish the proof of the main result, Theorem
6.1
.

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

By Lemma
6.2
and Lemma
6.3
, we evaluate the integral

(6.11)

\int_{\{\theta_{1}^{2}\delta_{1}^{2}+\theta_{2}^{2}\delta_{2}^{2}\leq 2\}}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{X_{J},X_{J}^{\prime}}e^{\langle(X+X^{\prime})_{J},u\rangle}|\mathbb{E}_{X_{I}}e^{4\pi i\langle\sum_{i=1}^{2}\theta_{i}\frac{(A_{n}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})^{-1}}{\mu_{1}(\lambda_{i})}(X-X^{\prime})_{J},X_{I}\rangle+2\langle X_{I},u\rangle}}|d\theta.

We consider separately the contribution to the integral from tuples
(J,X_{J},X_{J}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{F}
and
(J,X_{J},X_{J}^{\prime})\notin\mathcal{F}
. In the latter case, by triangle inequality, the contribution is bounded by

(6.12)

\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{J,X,X^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{F}^{c}}e^{\langle(X+X^{\prime})_{J},u\rangle+2\langle X_{I},u\rangle}}\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{J,X,X^{\prime}}[e^{\langle Y,2u\rangle}]^{1/2}\mathbb{P}_{J,X_{J},X_{J}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F}^{c})^{1/2}}\lesssim e^{-\Omega(n)},

where we write
Y=(X+X^{\prime})_{J}+2X_{I}
, and we use that the coordinates of
Y
are mean zero and subgaussian.
Then consider the case
(J,X_{J},X_{J}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{F}
. For each
i=1,2
we take
v_{i}=v_{i}(X):=\frac{(A_{n}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})^{-1}(X-X^{\prime})_{J}}{\mu_{1}(\lambda_{i})}
. Then by Lemma
6.4
, we have

(6.13)

|\mathbb{E}_{X_{I}}e^{2\pi i\langle X_{I},2\theta_{1}v_{1}+2\theta_{2}v_{2}\rangle+2\langle X_{I},u\rangle}|\lesssim\exp(-c\min_{r\in[1,c^{-1}]}\|2r(\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2})_{I}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{2}+c^{-1}\|u\|_{2}^{2}).

By assumption,
|\theta_{1}|,|\theta_{2}|
are smaller than
e^{c_{*}n/2}
. We always have
|v_{1}|\leq c^{-1}\sqrt{n}
and
|v_{2}|\leq c^{-1}\sqrt{n}
by definition of the two singular values
\mu_{1}(\lambda_{i}),\mu_{2}(\lambda_{i})
and on the event
\mathcal{F}_{2}
, so that
\|\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2}\|_{2}\leq e^{c_{\Sigma}n}
if
|\theta_{1}|,|\theta_{2}|\leq e^{c_{\Sigma}n/2}
.

There are two cases: if
\|(\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2})_{I}\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}
for any
|\theta_{1}|,|\theta_{2}|\leq e^{c_{\Sigma}n}
, then we can simply replace
\|(\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2})_{I}\|_{\mathbb{T}}=\|(\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2})_{I}\|_{2}
. Or if for some
|\theta_{1}|,|\theta_{2}|\leq e^{c_{\Sigma}n/2}
we have
\|(\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2})_{I}\|_{2}\geq 1
, then on the event
\mathcal{F}_{4}(A_{n})
, using
\|(\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2})_{I}\|_{2}\leq e^{c_{\Sigma}n}
,

\|(\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2})_{I}\|_{\mathbb{T}}=\|\frac{\|(\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2})_{I}\|_{2}(\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2})_{I}}{\|(\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2})_{I}\|_{2}}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq\min(\alpha|I|,\gamma\|(\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2})_{I}\|_{2})

by definition of the essential LCD. On the event
\mathcal{F}_{3}(A_{n})
, the vector
\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2}
is
(\delta,\rho)
-incompressible, so we can find some
C>0
depending only on
B
such that

\|(\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2})_{I}\|_{2}\geq C\|\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2}\|_{2}.

We take this back to the integral (
6.11
), consider separately
\gamma\|(\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2})_{I}\|_{2}\leq\alpha|I|
and the opposite case (where the right hand side of (
6.13
) is already exponentially small), and the contribution to the integral for pairs
(J,X_{J},X_{J}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{F}
is bounded by the integral of

\sqrt{\exp\left(\langle(X+X^{\prime})_{J},u\rangle-c\|\theta_{1}v_{1}+\theta_{2}v_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right)+e^{-\Omega(n)}}

over
\{\theta:\theta_{1}^{2}\delta_{1}^{2}+\theta_{2}^{2}\delta_{2}^{2}\leq 2\}
, which gives the claimed result by Lemma
6.2
.

∎

7.
Global properties of the spectrum

In this section we establish a few eigenvalue rigidity estimates for Wigner matrices. We make crucial use of the local semicircle law, as well as the super-exponential concentration of the empirical measure guaranteed by a finite log-Sobolev constant.

Recall that for any
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote by
\mu_{1}(\lambda_{i})\geq\mu_{2}(\lambda_{i})\cdots\geq\mu_{n}(\lambda_{i})\geq 0
the singular values of
(A_{n}-\lambda_{i}I)^{-1}
in decreasing order. Additionally, we define a modified norm
\|\cdot\|_{*}
for
(A_{n}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})^{-1}
via

(7.1)

\left\|(A_{n}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})^{-1}\right\|_{*}^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mu_{k}(\lambda_{i})^{2}(\log(1+k))^{2}.

The use of this norm will not be apparent until in Section
10.3
: before this we can safely replace
\|M\|_{*}
by
\|M\|_{HS}
. Informally, we can think of
\|M\|_{*}
as a biased version of
\|M\|_{HS}
, and that when
\|M\|_{*}
deviates significantly from
\|M\|_{HS}
, this would signify a macroscopic event taking place in the spectrum.

7.1.
Statement of main estimates

We first need the following two results that generalize Lemma 8.1 and 8.2 of
[
5
]
to nonzero eigenvalues in the bulk.

Lemma 7.1
.

For any
p>1
,
B>0
,
\kappa>0
and
\zeta\in\Gamma_{B}
, let
A\sim\operatorname{Sym}_{n}(\zeta)
. Then we can find a constant
C_{p}
depending on
B
,
p
and
\kappa
such that for any
\lambda_{i}\in[-(2-\kappa)\sqrt{n},(2-\kappa)\sqrt{n}]
we have, for all
k=1,2,\cdots,n
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\mu_{k}(\lambda_{i})\cdot k}\right)^{p}\right]\leq C_{p}.

Corollary 7.2
.

For a given
p>1
,
B>0
,
\kappa>0
and
\zeta\in\Gamma_{B}
,
A\in\operatorname{Sym}_{n}(\zeta)
. Then we can find a constant
C_{P}>0
depending on
B
,
p
,
\kappa
such that for any
\lambda_{i}\in[(-(2-\kappa)\sqrt{n},(2-\kappa)\sqrt{n}]
, we have

(7.2)

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\|(A_{n}-\lambda_{i}I_{n})^{-1}\|_{*}}{\mu_{1}(\lambda_{i})}\right)^{p}\right]\leq C_{p}.

We also need the following high-probability estimates for large values of
k
:

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
B>0
,
\zeta\in\Gamma_{B}
and
A_{n}\in\operatorname{Sym}_{n}(\zeta)
. Fix any
\kappa>0
. Fix two locations
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in[-(2-\kappa)\sqrt{n},(2-\kappa)\sqrt{n}]
and any
\sigma\in(0,1)
. Then for any
c_{0}>0
we can find constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}>0
depending only on
B
,
\kappa
(they do not depend on
c_{0}
) such that

(7.3)

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{C_{1}\sqrt{n}}{k}\leq\mu_{k}(\lambda_{i})\leq\frac{C_{2}\sqrt{n}}{k}\text{ for both $i\in\{1,2\}$ and all }c_{0}n^{\sigma}\leq k\leq C_{3}n\right)\geq 1-e^{-\Omega(n^{\sigma}/2)},

where the constant in the error term
\exp(-\Omega(n^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}))
depends also on
c_{0}>0
.

In particular, we can find
C_{4}>1
depending only on
\kappa
and
B
so that for any
c_{0}>0
,

\left(\mu_{\frac{k}{C_{4}}}(\lambda_{i})\geq{10}\mu_{k}(\lambda_{j})\text{ for all }c_{0}n^{\sigma}\leq k\leq C_{3}n\text{ and }\text{ for all }i,j\in\{1,2\}\right)\geq 1-e^{-\Omega(n^{\sigma/2})}.

Assuming further that the entries of
A
have a finite log-Sobolev constant, we can prove stronger concentration estimates:

Lemma 7.4
.

Given any
B>0
, a random variable
\zeta\in\Gamma_{B}
that has a finite,
n
-independent log-Sobolev constant, and a random matrix
A_{n}\sim\operatorname{Sym}_{n}(\zeta)
. Fix
\kappa>0
. Then we can find constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}>0
depending only on
\kappa
,
B
and the log-Sobolev constant (and not on
c_{0}
) such that, for any
c_{0}>0
and
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in[-(2-\kappa)\sqrt{n},(2-\kappa)\sqrt{n}]
,

(7.4)

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{C_{1}\sqrt{n}}{k}\leq\mu_{k}(\lambda_{i})\leq\frac{C_{2}\sqrt{n}}{k}\text{ for both $i\in\{1,2\}$ and all }c_{0}n\leq k\leq C_{3}n\right)\geq 1-e^{-\Omega(n)}.

In particular, we can find
C_{4}>1
depending only on
\kappa
,
B
and the log-Sobolev constant such that for any
c_{0}>0
and for each
\lambda_{i}
in the given interval,

\left(\mu_{\frac{k}{C_{4}}}(\lambda_{i})\geq{10}\mu_{k}(\lambda_{j})\text{ for all }c_{0}n\leq k\leq C_{3}n\text{ and }\text{ for all }i,j\in\{1,2\}\right)\geq 1-e^{-\Omega(n)}.

7.2.
Proofs via local semicircle law

The proofs of Lemma
7.1
, Corollary
7.2
and Corollary
7.3
rely on the following local semicircular law estimates for Wigner matrices by Erdös, Schlein and Yau
[
11
]
, Theorem 1.11. The Wigner semicircle law
\rho_{sc}
is defined as

(7.5)

\rho_{sc}(x):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{(4-x^{2})_{+}},\quad x\in\mathbb{R}.

Theorem 7.5
.

(
[
11
]
, Theorem 1.11.) Let
H\in\operatorname{Sym}_{n}(\zeta)
where
\zeta\in\Gamma_{B}
for some
B>0
. Let
\kappa>0
and consider
E\in[-2+\kappa,2-\kappa]
. Denote by
\mathcal{N}_{\eta^{*}}(E)=\mathcal{N}_{I^{*}}
the number of eigenvalues of
n^{-\frac{1}{2}}H
in
I^{*}:=[E-\eta^{*}/2,E+\eta^{*}/2]
. Then we can find a universal constant
c_{1}>0
and two constants
C
,
c>0
depending only on
\kappa
such that for any
\delta\leq c_{1}\kappa
we can find a constant
K_{\delta}
depending only on
\delta
such that

(7.6)

\mathbb{P}\left\{\left|\frac{\mathcal{N}_{\eta^{*}}(E)}{n\eta^{*}}-\rho_{sc}(E)\right|\geq\delta\right\}\leq Ce^{-c\delta^{2}\sqrt{n\eta^{*}}},

for all
\eta^{*}
satisfying
K_{\delta}/n\leq\eta^{*}\leq 1
and all
n\geq 2
.

Since
\rho_{sc}(x)
is maximal at
x=0
and decreases as
|x|
increases, we deduce the following immediate corollary:

Corollary 7.6
.

In the setting of Theorem
7.5
, for any
\kappa>0
we have the following two estimates uniform in all
E\in[-2+\kappa,2-\kappa]
: for any
\eta^{*}\in[C_{\ref{corollary425}}n^{-1},1]
,

(7.7)

\mathbb{P}\left\{\frac{\mathcal{N}_{\eta^{*}}(E)}{n\eta^{*}}\geq\pi\right\}\lesssim\exp(-c_{1}\sqrt{n\eta^{*}}),

and

(7.8)

\mathbb{P}\left\{\frac{\mathcal{N}_{\eta^{*}}(E)}{n\eta^{*}}\leq\frac{1}{4}\rho_{sc}(2-{\kappa})\right\}\lesssim\exp(-c_{1}\sqrt{n\eta^{*}}),

where
C_{\ref{corollary425}}
and
c_{1}
are constants that depend only on
\kappa>0
.

From this estimate we deduce two immediate corollaries.

Corollary 7.7
.

Fix
B>0
,
\zeta\in\Gamma_{B}
and
A_{n}\sim\operatorname{Sym}_{n}(\zeta)
. Fixing
\kappa>0
, then we can find positive constants
C_{\ref{corollary8.5}}
and
c
depending only on
\kappa
such that, for all
s\geq C_{\ref{corollary8.5}}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
that satisfy
sk\leq n
, uniformly for all
\lambda_{i}\in[-(2-\kappa)\sqrt{n},(2-\kappa)\sqrt{n}]
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\mu_{k}(\lambda_{i})\cdot k}\geq s\right)\lesssim\exp(-c(sk)^{\frac{1}{2}}).

We also have, uniformly for all
\lambda_{i}\in[-(2-\kappa)\sqrt{n},(2-\kappa)\sqrt{n}]
, for all
k\in\mathbb{N},

\mathbb{P}\left(\mu_{k}(\lambda_{i})\geq\frac{\sqrt{n}}{C_{\ref{corollary8.5}}k}\right)\lesssim\exp(-ck^{\frac{1}{2}}).

Proof.

Denote by
C_{\ref{corollary8.5}}:=\max(\pi,\frac{4}{\rho_{sc}(2-\kappa)},C_{\ref{corollary425}})
.
If
\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\mu_{k}(\lambda_{i})\cdot k}\geq s
, then
N_{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}A}(-skn^{-1}+\lambda_{i}n^{-}\frac{1}{2},skn^{-1}+\lambda_{i}n^{-}\frac{1}{2})\leq k
, where we use the symbol
N_{H}(I)
to denote the number of eigenvalues of
H
in the interval
I
.

Then the first claim follows by applying estimate
7.8
with
\eta_{*}=skn^{-1}\geq sn^{-1}\geq C_{\ref{corollary425}}n^{-1}
and noticing
s^{-1}\leq\frac{\rho_{sc}(2-\kappa)}{4}
. The second claim follows from applying the estimate
7.7
with
\eta_{*}=C_{\ref{corollary8.5}}kn^{-1}\geq C_{\ref{corollary8.5}}n^{-1}
and noticing
C_{\ref{corollary8.5}}\geq\pi
.
∎

Now we are in the place to prove Lemma
7.1
and Corollary
7.2
.

Proof of Lemma
7.1
.

From the tail estimate on
\|A_{n}\|_{op}
in Lemma
2.2
, we deduce that for any
k\geq n/C_{\ref{corollary8.5}}
and any
\lambda_{i}\in[-(2-\kappa)\sqrt{n},(2-\kappa)\sqrt{n}]
,

\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\mu_{k}(\lambda_{i})\cdot k}\right)^{p}\leq\mathbb{E}_{A_{n}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}(A_{n}+\lambda_{i}I_{n})\sqrt{n}}{k}\right)^{p}=O_{p}((n/k))^{p}=O_{p}(1).

Then for
k\leq n/C_{\ref{corollary8.5}}
, we consider separately the following three events

E_{1}^{i}=\left\{\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\mu_{k}(\lambda_{i})\cdot k}\leq C_{\ref{corollary8.5}}\right\},\quad E_{2}^{i}=\left\{\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\mu_{k}(\lambda_{i})\cdot k}\in[C_{\ref{corollary8.5}},\frac{n}{k}]\right\},\quad E_{3}^{i}=\left\{\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\mu_{k}(\lambda_{i})\cdot k}\geq\frac{n}{k}\right\}

and bound

(7.9)

\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\mu_{k}(\lambda_{i})\cdot k}\right)^{p}\leq C_{\ref{corollary8.5}}^{p}+\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\mu_{k}(\lambda_{i})\cdot k}\right)^{p}\mathbf{1}_{E_{2}^{i}}+\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\mu_{k}(\lambda_{i})\cdot k}\right)^{p}\mathbf{1}_{E_{3}^{i}}.

For the second term, we apply Corollary
7.7
to bound

\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\mu_{k}(\lambda_{i})\cdot k}\right)^{p}\lesssim\int_{C_{\ref{corollary8.5}}}^{n/k}ps^{p-1}e^{-c\sqrt{sk}}ds=O_{p}(1).

For the third term, since
n/k\geq C_{\ref{corollary8.5}}
, we apply Corollary
7.7
with
s=n/k
and deduce that
\mathbb{P}(E_{3}^{i})\lesssim e^{-c\sqrt{n}}
. Then applying the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}
